divided by a
trajectory of type
ij
. Then
\psi_{i}^{\vartheta}
and

\tilde{\psi}_{i}^{\vartheta}
are related by the connection formulas

\displaystyle\psi_{i}^{\vartheta}\longmapsto\tilde{\psi}_{i}^{\vartheta}=\psi_{i}^{\vartheta}+c_{ij}\,\psi_{j}^{\vartheta}\qquad\mbox{and}\qquad\psi_{j}^{\vartheta}\longmapsto\tilde{\psi}_{j}^{\vartheta}=\psi_{j}^{\vartheta}\ ,

(4.63)

where the functions
c_{ij}
are known as Stokes multipliers.
Note that these connection formulas are precisely of the form of the
unipotent transformations from (
2.18
). Hence the exact WKB
solutions
\psi_{i}^{\vartheta}
provide a basis of sections which
abelianize the flat
SL(2,\mathbb{C})

\epsilon
-connection
\nabla^{\rm oper}_{\epsilon}
. The corresponding almost flat
GL(1,\mathbb{C})

\epsilon
-connection
\nabla_{\epsilon}^{\rm ab}
has the explicit form

\displaystyle\big(\epsilon\,\partial_{z}-S_{i}^{\vartheta}\big)\psi_{i}^{\vartheta}=0\ .

(4.64)

Example 24
.

Perhaps the simplest example of the exact WKB method is provided by
the (actual) Schrödinger equation for the complex harmonic oscillator

\displaystyle{\rm D}_{\epsilon}\psi(z)=\epsilon^{2}\,\psi^{\prime\prime}(z)-\big(z^{2}+m\big)\,\psi(z)=0

(4.65)

from Example
19

[
KT05
]
. Note that the differential equation (
4.65
) is invariant under
\epsilon\mapsto-\epsilon
and
z\mapsto-z
. We fix
{\rm arg}(\epsilon)
. If we take
\vartheta={\rm arg}(\epsilon)=\frac{\pi}{2}
and
m>0
, then the corresponding spectral
network
\mathscr{W}=\mathscr{W}(u,\vartheta)
is shown in
Figure
3
.
32
32

32

Changing
{\rm arg}(\epsilon)
rotates
the trajectories at infinity; changing
\epsilon
and
m
, while
leaving
{\rm arg}(m/\epsilon)
invariant, rotates the entire
network.
Let us choose these values for convenience. The
formal series solutions to (
4.65
) are of the form

\displaystyle\psi_{1}^{\rm for}(z)

\displaystyle={\mathrm{e}}^{-t^{2}/4}\,t^{\mu}\,\sum_{n=0}^{\infty}\,(-1)^{n}\,\frac{\mu\,(\mu-1)\cdots(\mu-2n+1)}{n!\,2^{n}\,t^{2n}}\ ,

\displaystyle\psi_{2}^{\rm for}(z)

\displaystyle={\mathrm{e}}^{\,t^{2}/4}\,t^{-\mu-1}\,\sum_{n=0}^{\infty}\,\frac{(\mu+1)\,(\mu+2)\cdots(\mu+2n)}{n!\,2^{n}\,t^{2n}}\ ,

(4.66)

where

\displaystyle t=\sqrt{\frac{2}{\epsilon}}\,z\qquad\mbox{and}\qquad\mu=-\frac{m}{2\,\epsilon}-\frac{1}{2}\ ,

(4.67)

and we assume
\mu\notin\mathbb{Z}
. The formal series

\psi_{1}^{\rm for}(z)
decreases fastest along the line

t=\sqrt{2/\epsilon}\,z\in\mathbb{R}
, while the formal series

\psi_{2}^{\rm for}(z)
decreases fastest along the line

t=\sqrt{2/\epsilon}\,z\in\,{\mathrm{i}}\,\mathbb{R}
. These are the trajectories
going to infinity at angles
\frac{\pi}{4}
and
\frac{3\pi}{4}
in
Figure
3
; see also
Figure
36
.

\begin{overpic}[Stokes_Rays.png]
\put(21.0,62.0){$\psi_{2}^{\rm for}$}
\put(75.0,62.0){$\psi_{1}^{\rm for}$}
\put(58.0,40.0){$\frac{\pi}{4}$}
\put(41.0,45.0){$\frac{3\pi}{4}$}
\end{overpic}

Figure 36:
Stokes rays for the Schrödinger equation (
4.65
) with arg
(\epsilon)=\pi/2
.

Let us therefore consider the Borel sums of
\psi_{1}^{\rm for}(z)
and

\psi_{2}^{\rm for}(z)
. The Borel transform of the series

\displaystyle f(t)=t^{\mu}\,\sum_{n=0}^{\infty}\,(-1)^{n}\,\frac{\mu\,(\mu-1)\cdots(\mu-2n+1)}{n!\,2^{n}\,t^{2n}}

(4.68)

is given by

\displaystyle\mathcal{B}[f](s)

\displaystyle=\sum_{n=0}^{\infty}\,(-1)^{n}\,\frac{\mu\,(\mu-1)\cdots(\mu-2n+1)}{n!\,2^{n}\,\Gamma(-\mu+2n)}\,s^{2n-\mu-1}

\displaystyle=\sum_{n=0}^{\infty}\,\frac{(-1)^{n}}{n!\,2^{n}\,\Gamma(-\mu)}\,s^{2n-\mu-1}

\displaystyle=\frac{s^{-\mu-1}}{\Gamma(-\mu)}\,{\mathrm{e}}^{-s^{2}/2}\ ,

(4.69)

where the weight
\Gamma(-\mu+2n)
in (
4.69
) is
determined by the prefactor
t^{\mu}
in
\psi_{1}^{\rm for}(z)
. The
Borel sum of
f
for
t>0
is thus

\displaystyle\mathcal{S}_{0}f(t)=\frac{1}{\Gamma(-\mu)}\,\int_{0}^{\infty}\,\mathrm{d}s\ {\mathrm{e}}^{-t\,s-\frac{s^{2}}{2}}\,s^{-\mu-1}\ .

(4.70)

The holomorphic function

\displaystyle\psi_{1}(z)={\mathrm{e}}^{-t^{2}/4}\,\mathcal{S}_{0}f(t)=D_{\mu}\Big(\sqrt{\frac{2}{\epsilon}}\,z\Big)

(4.71)

is an integral representation of the Weber parabolic cylinder
function. This is indeed an exact solution of the Schrödinger
equation (
4.65
). The Borel sum
\psi_{1}(z)
can be
analytically continued to
{\rm arg}(z)\in[-\frac{\pi}{4},\frac{3\pi}{4}]
. Similarly, for
{\mathrm{i}}\,t=\sqrt{2/\epsilon}\,{\mathrm{i}}\,z<0
the Borel sum
\psi_{2}(z)
of the formal series
\psi_{2}^{\rm for}(z)
coincides with the Weber parabolic cylinder function

\displaystyle\psi_{2}(z)={\mathrm{e}}^{-\frac{\pi\,{\mathrm{i}}}{2}\,(\mu+1)}\,D_{-\mu-1}\Big(-\sqrt{\frac{2}{\epsilon}}\,{\mathrm{i}}\,z\Big)\ ,

(4.72)

and it can be analytically continued to
{\rm arg}(z)\in[\frac{\pi}{4},\frac{5\pi}{4}]
.

For
t=\sqrt{2/\epsilon}\,z<0
the Borel sum of
\psi_{1}^{\rm for}(z)
coincides with

\displaystyle\psi_{3}(z)={\mathrm{e}}^{\,\pi\,{\mathrm{i}}\,\mu}\,D_{\mu}\Big(-\sqrt{\frac{2}{\epsilon}}\,z\Big)\ ,

(4.73)

and it can be analytically continued to
{\rm arg}(z)\in[\frac{3\pi}{4},\frac{7\pi}{4}]
. For
{\mathrm{i}}\,t=\sqrt{2/\epsilon}\,{\mathrm{i}}\,z>0
the Borel sum of
\psi_{2}^{\rm for}(z)
is equal to

\displaystyle\psi_{4}(z)={\mathrm{e}}^{-\frac{\pi\,{\mathrm{i}}}{2}\,(3\,\mu-1)}\,D_{-\mu-1}\Big(\sqrt{\frac{2}{\epsilon}}\,{\mathrm{i}}\,z\Big)\ ,

(4.74)

which can be analytically continued to
{\rm arg}(z)\in[-\frac{3\pi}{4},\frac{\pi}{4}]
. We thus find four Stokes sectors at
infinity, with bases of exact solutions as shown in Figure
37
.

\begin{overpic}[Stokes_Rays.png]
\put(75.0,63.0){$\psi_{1}=D_{\mu}\Big(\sqrt{\frac{2}{\epsilon}}\,z\Big)$}
\put(-5.0,63.0){$\psi_{2}={\mathrm{e}}^{-\frac{\pi\,{\mathrm{i}}}{2}\,(\mu+1)}\,D_{-\mu-1}\Big(-\sqrt{\frac{2}{\epsilon}}\,{\mathrm{i}}\,z\Big)$}
\put(-5.0,5.0){$\psi_{3}={\mathrm{e}}^{\,\pi\,{\mathrm{i}}\,\mu}\,D_{\mu}\Big(-\sqrt{\frac{2}{\epsilon}}\,z\Big)$}
\put(65.0,5.0){$\psi_{4}={\mathrm{e}}^{-\frac{\pi\,{\mathrm{i}}}{2}\,(3\,\mu-1)}\,D_{-\mu-1}\Big(\sqrt{\frac{2}{\epsilon}}\,{\mathrm{i}}\,z\Big)$}
\put(44.5,17.0){$\left(\psi_{3},\psi_{4}\right)$}
\put(44.5,52.0){$\left(\psi_{1},\psi_{2}\right)$}
\put(30.0,34.5){$\left(\psi_{3},\psi_{2}\right)$}
\put(58.0,34.5){$\left(\psi_{1},\psi_{4}\right)$}
\end{overpic}

Figure 37:
Local basis of solutions at infinity for the Schrödinger equation (
4.65
) with arg
(\epsilon)=\pi/2
.

The exact solutions
\psi_{l}(z)
and
\psi_{l+2}(z)
are related across
the Stokes ray labelled by
\psi_{l+1}(z)
by the connection formula

\displaystyle\psi_{l}=\psi_{l+2}+c_{l,l+2}\,\psi_{l+1}\ ,

(4.75)

where the Stokes multipliers

\displaystyle c_{l,l+2}=\frac{\text{Wr}(\psi_{l},\psi_{l+2})}{\text{Wr}(\psi_{l+1},\psi_{l+2})}

(4.76)

may be obtained as a ratio of Wronskians of
\psi_{l}(z)
and

\psi_{l+2}(z)
, and of
\psi_{l+1}(z)
and
\psi_{l+2}(z)
. This
yields

\displaystyle\psi_{1}(z)

\displaystyle=\psi_{3}(z)+\frac{\sqrt{2\pi}\,{\mathrm{e}}^{\,\pi\,{\mathrm{i}}\,(\mu+1)}}{\Gamma(-\mu)}\,\psi_{2}(z)\ ,

\displaystyle\psi_{2}(z)

\displaystyle=\psi_{4}(z)-\frac{\sqrt{2\pi}\,{\mathrm{i}}\,{\mathrm{e}}^{-2\,\pi\,{\mathrm{i}}\,\mu}}{\Gamma(\mu+1)}\,\psi_{3}(z)\ ,

\displaystyle\psi_{3}(z)

\displaystyle={\mathrm{e}}^{\,2\pi\,{\mathrm{i}}\,\mu}\,\psi_{1}(z)-\frac{\sqrt{2\pi}\,{\mathrm{e}}^{\,\pi\,{\mathrm{i}}\,(3\,\mu+1)}}{\Gamma(-\mu)}\,\psi_{4}(z)\ ,

\displaystyle\psi_{4}(z)

\displaystyle={\mathrm{e}}^{-2\pi\,{\mathrm{i}}\,\mu}\,\psi_{2}(z)+\frac{\sqrt{2\pi}\,{\mathrm{i}}\,{\mathrm{e}}^{-2\pi\,{\mathrm{i}}\,\mu}}{\Gamma(\mu+1)}\,\psi_{1}(z)\ ,

(4.77)

where we used
\psi_{5}(z)={\mathrm{e}}^{\,2\pi\,{\mathrm{i}}\,\mu}\,\psi_{1}(z)
and

\psi_{6}(z)={\mathrm{e}}^{-2\pi\,{\mathrm{i}}\,\mu}\,\psi_{2}(z)
. These formulas
indeed agree with the classical connection
formulas for parabolic cylinder functions
(see
[
Olv+10
, Chapter 12]
)

\displaystyle D_{\mu}(t)

\displaystyle={\mathrm{e}}^{\mp\,\pi\,{\mathrm{i}}\,\mu}\,D_{\mu}(-t)+\frac{\sqrt{2\pi}}{\Gamma(-\mu)}\,{\mathrm{e}}^{\mp\,\frac{\pi\,{\mathrm{i}}}{2}\,(\mu+1)}\,D_{-\mu-1}(\pm\,{\mathrm{i}}\,t)\ ,

\displaystyle D_{-\mu-1}(\pm\,{\mathrm{i}}\,t)

\displaystyle=-{\mathrm{e}}^{\mp\,\pi\,{\mathrm{i}}\,\mu}\,D_{-\mu-1}(\mp\,{\mathrm{i}}\,t)+\frac{\sqrt{2\pi}}{\Gamma(\mu+1)}\,{\mathrm{e}}^{\mp\,\frac{\pi\,{\mathrm{i}}}{2}\,\mu}\,D_{\mu}(t)\ .

(4.78)

Since the connection formulas (
4.77
) are of the form of the unipotent
transformations (
4.63
) or (
2.18
), the sections

\displaystyle s_{l}(z)=\begin{pmatrix}-\epsilon\,\psi_{l}^{\prime}(z)\\
\psi_{l}(z)\end{pmatrix}

(4.79)

determine a distinguished
\mathscr{W}
-abelianization of the Schrödinger oper
(
4.65
). In particular, the exact WKB method has provided
us with the framing data
s_{l}
.

Now that we have found a distinguished abelianization for the flat

\epsilon
-connection
\nabla_{\epsilon}^{\rm oper}
, for
{\rm arg}(\epsilon)=\vartheta
, with respect to
the WKB spectral network
\mathscr{W}(u,\vartheta)
, we can consider the
spectral coordinates associated to this abelianization. Recall that
for a one-cycle
\gamma
on
\Sigma
, the corresponding spectral
coordinate is defined by

\displaystyle\mathcal{X}^{\mathscr{W}(u,\vartheta)}_{\gamma}(\nabla_{\epsilon}^{\rm oper})={\rm Hol}_{\gamma}\nabla_{\epsilon}^{\rm ab}\ .

(4.80)

In exact WKB analysis, the logarithm of this spectral coordinate, for
{\rm arg}(\epsilon)=\vartheta
, is known as the Voros symbol
V_{\gamma}(\epsilon)
. It follows from the discussion above that the spectral coordinate
\mathcal{X}^{\mathscr{W}(u,\vartheta)}_{\gamma}(\nabla_{\epsilon}^{\rm oper})
, now with
{\rm arg}(\epsilon)
a free parameter, has good
asymptotics when
\epsilon\to 0
in the half-plane with
{\rm Re}({\mathrm{e}}^{-{\mathrm{i}}\,\vartheta}\,\epsilon)\geq 0
. It is given by

\exp\big(\Pi_{\gamma}(\epsilon)\big)
, where

\displaystyle\Pi_{\gamma}(\epsilon)=\frac{1}{\epsilon}\,\oint_{\gamma}\,S_{i}^{\rm for}(z;\epsilon)\,\mathrm{d}z

(4.81)

is called a quantum period.
33
33

33

This is also the conformal
limit of the
\zeta\to 0
asymptotics of
\mathcal{X}_{\gamma}(\mathcal{A})
, where
\mathcal{A}

is the flat connection from
(
4.19
)
[
GMN13b
]
.

We should point out one subtlety. The exact WKB analysis, just like abelianization, is not defined at a critical phase
\vartheta_{\rm c}
when the corresponding network
\mathscr{W}(u,\vartheta_{\rm c})
develops one or more saddle trajectories. Again, in parallel to our approach with abelianization, one may apply the exact WKB method to the resolutions
\mathscr{W}(u,\vartheta_{\rm c}^{\pm})
of the critical network. In exact WKB analysis this is called lateral Borel resummation, while the average Voros symbol
V_{\gamma}^{\rm c}=\frac{1}{2}\,\big(V_{\gamma}^{+}+V_{\gamma}^{-}\big)
is said to be obtained using median summation.

Suppose we keep the vacuum
u
fixed while varying the phase
\vartheta
. As long as we do not cross any BPS ray (a critical phase where the spectral network
\mathscr{W}(u,\vartheta)
develops a saddle trajectory), the spectral coordinate
\mathcal{X}^{\mathscr{W}(u,\vartheta)}_{\gamma}(\nabla_{\epsilon}^{\rm oper})
or Voros symbol
V_{\gamma}(\epsilon)
, with
{\rm arg}(\epsilon)=\vartheta
, is an analytic function of
\epsilon
.

However, the spectral coordinate
\mathcal{X}^{\mathscr{W}(u,\vartheta)}_{\gamma}(\nabla_{\epsilon}^{\rm oper})
or Voros symbol
V_{\gamma}(\epsilon)
, with
{\rm arg}(\epsilon)=\vartheta
, will have a jump discontinuity across a BPS ray. From the perspective of abelianization this is because the topology of the spectral network changes, while in the exact WKB method this occurs because of the need to move a contour of integration across a singularity in the Borel plane. This jump will generally be of the form of a Kontsevich-Soibelman cluster transformation associated to the BPS states supported by the BPS wall.

Hence the spectral coordinates
\mathcal{X}^{\mathscr{W}(u,\vartheta)}_{\gamma}(\nabla_{\epsilon}^{\rm oper})
or Voros symbols
V_{\gamma}(\epsilon)
, with
{\rm arg}(\epsilon)=\vartheta
, are piecewise analytic functions on the parameter space. Yet, for a fixed phase
\vartheta
, these coordinates may be analytically continued to analytic functions on the whole
\epsilon
-plane. The resulting analytically continued functions agree with the spectral coordinates
\mathcal{X}_{\gamma}^{\mathscr{W}}
obtained by the abelianization method, evaluated at the flat
\epsilon
-connection
\nabla_{\epsilon}^{\rm oper}
, where we fix the isotopy class
\mathscr{W}
of the spectral network
\mathscr{W}(u,\vartheta)
.

For further background, details and examples we refer
to
[
HN20
]

and
[
HK18
, Section 11]
. A particularly interesting
feature is that the spectral coordinates

\mathcal{X}^{\mathscr{W}(u,\vartheta)}_{\gamma}(\nabla_{\epsilon}^{\rm oper})
or Voros periods
V_{\gamma}(\epsilon)
, for
{\rm arg}(\epsilon)=\vartheta
, may also be characterized as
solutions to integral equations in the
\epsilon
-plane, called
TBA equations. The general form of these equations has been
formulated in
[
Gai14
]
, and derived (from reasonable
analytic assumptions) in
[
IMS19
]
, while more specifics and
examples are found
in
[
HN20
,
GGM20
,
GHN21
]
where the various methods for computing quantum periods are compared numerically. Similar equations in the context of
topological string theory appear in the work of
Bridgeland
[
Bri19
]
.

Example 25
.

Consider the one-parameter family of
\epsilon
-opers

\nabla_{\epsilon}^{\rm oper}(u)
from
Example
22
with
u\in\mathbb{R}
and
{\rm arg}\,\epsilon=0=\vartheta
. For these choices the spectral
network
\mathscr{W}(u,\vartheta)
is isotopic to the circular Fenchel-Nielsen
network from Figure
11
. We introduce one-cycles
A
,
B
and
C
with
C=-A-B
on the spectral cover
\Sigma
as shown in Figure
38
.

\begin{overpic}[E6Cycles.png]
\put(50.0,47.0){{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}$A$}}
\put(42.0,16.0){{\color[rgb]{0.1953,0.1953,1}$B$}}
\put(22.0,39.0){{\color[rgb]{0,0.6875,0}$C$}}
\end{overpic}

Figure 38:
Three one-cycles
A
,
B
and
C=-A-B
on
\Sigma
.

The spectral coordinates
\mathcal{X}_{A}
and
\mathcal{X}_{B}
(for the distinguished WKB
abelianization), evaluated at the family of
\epsilon
-opers
\nabla_{\epsilon}^{\rm oper}(u)
, have an asymptotic expansion determined by the Riccati equation, as
\epsilon\to 0
in the
half-plane with
{\rm Re}({\mathrm{e}}^{-{\mathrm{i}}\,\vartheta}\,\epsilon)\geq 0
. This has been verified numerically in
[
HN20
, Section 6.7]
. The Riccati equation in this case is found by making the ansatz

\displaystyle\psi(z;\epsilon)=\exp\bigg(\sum_{n=-1}^{\infty}\,\epsilon^{n}\ \int_{0}^{z}\,S_{n}(z^{\prime})\,\mathrm{d}z^{\prime}\bigg)

(4.82)

for the solutions of the
T_{3}
-equation

\displaystyle\epsilon^{3}\,\frac{\partial^{3}\psi(z)}{\partial z^{3}}+\epsilon\,q_{2}(z)\,\frac{\partial\psi(z)}{\partial z}+q_{3}(z)\,\psi(z)=0\ ,

(4.83)

with

\displaystyle q_{2}(z)=\frac{9\,z}{(z^{3}-1)^{2}}\qquad\mbox{and}\qquad q_{3}(z)=\frac{u}{(z^{3}-1)^{2}}+\frac{\epsilon}{2}\,q_{2}^{\prime}(z)\ .

(4.84)

This yields

\displaystyle S_{-1}^{(i)}=-\frac{\omega^{i}\,u^{1/3}}{(z^{3}-1)^{2/3}}\ ,\quad S_{0}^{(i)}=\frac{2\,z^{2}}{z^{3}-1}\ ,\quad S_{1}^{(i)}=\frac{\omega^{-i}\,u^{-1/3}\,z}{3\,(z^{3}-1)^{4/3}}\ ,\quad\dots

(4.85)

for
i=0,1,2
, where we fixed the labelling of the sheets
(i)
by choosing
S_{-1}^{(i)}=-\omega^{i}\,u^{1/3}
at
z=0
, with
\omega={\mathrm{e}}^{\,2\pi\,{\mathrm{i}}/3}
. The spectral coordinate
\mathcal{X}_{A}
then has an asymptotic expansion

\displaystyle\mathcal{X}_{A}(\epsilon)\sim\exp\big(\Pi_{A}(\epsilon)\big)

\displaystyle=\exp\bigg(\sum_{n=-1}^{\infty}\,\epsilon^{n}\ \oint_{A}\,S_{n}(z^{\prime})\,\mathrm{d}z^{\prime}\bigg)

\displaystyle=\exp\bigg(\sum_{n=-1}^{\infty}\,\epsilon^{n}\,\Big(\int_{1}^{\omega}\,S_{n}^{(2)}(z^{\prime})\,\mathrm{d}z^{\prime}+\int_{\omega}^{1}\,S_{n}^{(3)}(z^{\prime})\,\mathrm{d}z^{\prime}\Big)\bigg)\ .

(4.86)

With
t=u^{1/3}/\epsilon
, we find

\displaystyle\Pi_{A}(t)

\displaystyle=-\frac{\Gamma(\frac{1}{3})\,\Gamma(\frac{1}{6})}{2^{2/3}\,\sqrt{\pi}}\,t-\frac{3\,\sqrt{\pi}\,\Gamma(\frac{5}{3})}{2^{1/3}\,\Gamma(\frac{1}{6})}\,t^{-1}+\frac{11\,\Gamma(\frac{1}{3})\,\Gamma(\frac{7}{6})}{270\cdot 2^{2/3}\,\sqrt{\pi}}\,t^{-5}

\displaystyle\quad\,-\frac{83\,\sqrt{\pi}\,\Gamma(\frac{5}{3})}{2268\cdot 2^{1/3}\,\Gamma(\frac{7}{6})}\,t^{-7}+\frac{44857\,\Gamma(\frac{1}{3})\,\Gamma(\frac{7}{6})}{96228\cdot 2^{2/3}\,\sqrt{\pi}}\,t^{-11}+\cdots\ .

(4.87)

Similarly, the spectral coordinates
\mathcal{X}_{B}
and
\mathcal{X}_{C}
have asymptotic expansions determined by the quantum periods
\Pi_{C}(\omega^{2}\,t)=\Pi_{B}(\omega\,t)=\Pi_{A}(t)
.

A challenge for the interested reader is to try to find the
Borel sum
\mathcal{S}_{\vartheta}\mathcal{X}_{A}(t)
of the quantum period
\Pi_{A}(t)
in the
direction
\vartheta
. In
[
HN20
, Section 6.7]
it is explained
how to obtain
\mathcal{X}_{A}(t)
numerically, by both solving the
abelianization problem for the
T_{3}
-equation as well as by writing
down and approximating suitable integral equations. An exact answer is not
presently known (and might require the definition of a new sort of
“special function”).

5
The Nekrasov-Rosly-Shatashvili Correspondence

In this section we formulate a recipe for computing the effective twisted superpotential
\widetilde{\mathcal{W}}^{\text{eff}}
of an
{\mathcal{N}}=2

field theory
{\sf T}_{K}[C,\mathcal{D}]
of class
\mathcal{S}
in the

\frac{1}{2}\Omega
-background, geometrically in terms of spectral
coordinates and opers. This recipe is based on a proposal of
Nekrasov, Rosly and Shatashvili (NRS), who conjectured that
\widetilde{\mathcal{W}}^{\text{eff}}
is
essentially the difference between the generating functions of two holomorphic
Lagrangian subspaces of the associated moduli space
\mathscr{M}_{\rm flat}(C,SL(K,\mathbb{C}))
of flat
SL(K,\mathbb{C})
connections on
C
, in the
appropriate Darboux coordinates on
\mathscr{M}_{\rm flat}(C,SL(K,\mathbb{C}))

[
NRS11
]
; this conjecture is formulated in Section
5.1
. Evidence for this
conjecture has so far been given for the conformal
SU(2)
and
SU(3)

theories coupled to four and six
hypermultiplets
[
NRS11
,
TV15
,
HK18
,
JN18
]
.

Here we will consider two new examples, the rank one
Argyres-Douglas theory encountered in Example
14
and the
pure
SU(2)
theory introduced in Section
3.1
. The
novel ingredients involved are the new Fenchel-Nielsen type
coordinates found in Section
2
, see
Examples
10
,
11
and
12
, respectively.
In Section
5.2
we formulate the
recipe for
computing
\widetilde{\mathcal{W}}^{\text{eff}}
geometrically. This has been tested in
[
HK18
]
for the conformal
SU(2)
and
SU(3)
examples. Here we test it for the rank one Argyres-Douglas theory and the weakly coupled pure
SU(2)
theory in Sections
5.3
and
5.4
, whereas in Section
5.5
we use it to propose a new expansion of

\widetilde{\mathcal{W}}^{\text{eff}}
for the
E_{6}
Minahan-Nemeschansky theory discussed in Section
3.2
. In Section
5.6
we give more background
information and a string theory derivation of the NRS
proposal, mostly following the treatment of Nekrasov and Witten in
[
NW10
]
, while in Section
5.7
we discuss the conjecture with more general boundary conditions.

5.1
The NRS Conjecture

In Section
4
we have encountered the parameter
\epsilon

in two different contexts. Firstly, as deformation parameter of the

\frac{1}{2}\Omega
-background in Section
4.1
, and secondly as
a quantization parameter
\epsilon=\zeta/R
in the Hitchin moduli
space
\mathscr{M}_{\textrm{\tiny H}}
in Sections
4.2
–
4.4
. In
[
NS10
]
these two
instances of
\epsilon
are related. It is proposed that the low
energy physics of the
{\mathcal{N}}=2
theory
{\sf T}_{K}[C,\mathcal{D}]
of class
\mathcal{S}

in the
\frac{1}{2}\Omega
-background
\mathbb{R}_{\epsilon}^{2}\times\mathbb{R}^{2}
is
described by quantization of the Hitchin integrable system

(\mathscr{M}_{\textrm{\tiny H}}(C,SU(K)),J_{0})
(or more generally
(\mathscr{M}_{\textrm{\tiny H}}(C,{}^{\textrm{\tiny L}}G),J_{0})
where
{}^{\textrm{\tiny L}}G
is the Langlands dual of the
gauge group
G
).

In particular, Nekrasov and Shatashvili argue
that the generators of the ‘twisted ring’ of the effective
two-dimensional
{\mathcal{N}}=(2,2)
theory
{\sf T}_{\epsilon}
may be identified
with the quantum Hamiltonians, and that its supersymmetric vacua may
be identified with the ‘Bethe states’ of the quantum integrable
system. The latter claim implies that the effective twisted
superpotential
\widetilde{\mathcal{W}}^{\text{eff}}
of the
{\mathcal{N}}=(2,2)
theory
{\sf T}_{\epsilon}

corresponds to the Yang-Yang function
Y
of the quantum
Hitchin system. That is,

\displaystyle\widetilde{\mathcal{W}}^{\text{eff}}(a)=Y(\nu)\ ,

(5.1)

where the vacuum expectation values
\sigma_{i}=a_{i}
of the scalars in
the twisted chiral superfields
\bm{\Sigma}
are identified with
the spectral parameters (or rapidities)
\nu_{i}
.

This proposal was made more tangible in
[
NRS11
]
. It was conjectured that the
effective twisted superpotential
\widetilde{\mathcal{W}}^{\text{eff}}
corresponds to the difference
of two generating functions of holomorphic Lagrangian submanifolds of the
moduli space

\mathscr{M}_{\rm flat}^{\epsilon}(C,SL(K,\mathbb{C}))
in appropriate Darboux coordinates, and
that the supersymmetric vacua may be identified with the intersection
points of these holomorphic Lagrangian submanifolds. Let us explain this in a few more words.

Given any system of holomorphic Darboux
coordinates
(x,y)
on
\mathscr{M}^{\epsilon}_{\rm flat}(C,SL(K,\mathbb{C}))
,

\displaystyle\{x_{i},y^{j}\}=\delta_{i}^{j}\ ,

(5.2)

we can define a generating function
\mathcal{W}
of any holomorphic Lagrangian
submanifold of the moduli space
\mathscr{M}_{\rm flat}^{\epsilon}(C,SL(K,\mathbb{C}))
in this coordinate chart through the equation

y^{i}=\frac{\partial\mathcal{W}(x)}{\partial x_{i}}\ ,

(5.3)

which uniquely determines it up to an
x
-independent
function.

As can be anticipated from Section
4
, one holomorphic Lagrangian submanifold is the brane of
\epsilon
-opers
\mathscr{L}_{\epsilon}^{\rm oper}
. As explained in Section
4.3
, this submanifold may be characterized as the quantization of the Coulomb branch
\mathscr{B}
with quantization parameter
\epsilon
. The second holomorphic Lagrangian submanifold
corresponds to a boundary condition in the
{\mathcal{N}}=(2,2)
theory
{\sf T}_{\epsilon}
at infinity in
\mathbb{R}^{2}_{\epsilon}
. It might be surprising that such a boundary condition is needed, given that the original computation of the four-dimensional Nekrasov partition function did not require one. However, as already observed in
[
NS10
]
, further emphasized in
[
NW10
]
, and as we will expand on later, the two-dimensional perspective reveals that the definition of
{\sf T}_{\epsilon}
requires more data than just the choice of a four-dimensional theory
\sf T
and a parameter
\epsilon
.

In
[
NRS11
]
the appropriate Darboux
coordinates are described in the example of the conformal
SU(2)
theory
coupled to four hypermultiplets (which is generalized to the conformal

SU(K)
theory coupled to
2K
hypermultiplets
in
[
JN18
]
). In this case the appropriate Darboux
coordinates are basically the complex
Fenchel-Nielsen coordinates described in
Example
12
.
34
34

34

These are called Darboux
coordinates rather than complex Fenchel-Nielsen coordinates
in
[
NRS11
]
because the shift ambiguity in the
Fenchel-Nielsen twist coordinate is fixed in the NRS Darboux
coordinates.
We will outline the string theory derivation of the NRS conjecture (based on
[
NW10
]
) in
Section
5.6
. A gauge theory proof of the conjecture, in the
conformal
SU(2)
and
SU(3)
examples, was given
in
[
JN18
]
, whereas a conformal field theory perspective
on the conjecture was offered in
[
TV15
]
.

5.2
A Geometric Recipe

We shall now make some additional remarks, with the goal of turning the NRS conjecture into a concrete recipe for computing the effective twisted
superpotential
\widetilde{\mathcal{W}}^{\text{eff}}
geometrically for any
{\mathcal{N}}=2
theory
{\sf T}={\sf T}_{K}[C,\mathcal{D}]
of class
\mathcal{S}
. Recall from Section
4.1
that the effective twisted superpotential
\widetilde{\mathcal{W}}^{\text{eff}}(a;\epsilon)
may be obtained as the Nekrasov-Shatashvili limit

\displaystyle\widetilde{\mathcal{W}}^{\text{eff}}(a;\epsilon)=\lim_{\epsilon_{2}\to 0}\,\epsilon_{2}\,\log Z^{\rm Nek}(a;\epsilon_{1}=\epsilon,\epsilon_{2})

(5.4)

of the Nekrasov partition function
Z^{\rm Nek}(a;\epsilon_{1},\epsilon_{2})
. The latter partition function may be computed from first principles for any
{\mathcal{N}}=2
theory
\sf T
with a Lagrangian formulation (that is, in terms of gauge fields possibly coupled to matter fields).

However, recall that an
{\mathcal{N}}=2
theory
\sf T
may have distinct Lagrangian descriptions in different regions of its moduli space (this is the generalized S-duality discussed in Section
3.2
), or it may not even have a Lagrangian formulation at all (see as well Section
3.2
). The characterization of the corresponding two-dimensional theory
{\sf T}_{\epsilon}
(the theory
\sf T
in the
\frac{1}{2}\Omega
-background), as well as the explicit expression for
\widetilde{\mathcal{W}}^{\text{eff}}(a;\epsilon)
, therefore depends on more data than just the theory
\sf T
and the parameter
\epsilon
. In particular, it matters in which region of the moduli space we are considering
\sf T
. For example, the standard Lagrangian description of the pure
SU(2)
theory is only valid in the weak coupling region of its moduli space, and is distinct from the dual weakly coupled description near its strong coupling points. We thus obtain two distinct two-dimensional
{\mathcal{N}}=(2,2)
theories

{\sf T}_{\epsilon}^{\rm w}
and
{\sf T}_{\epsilon}^{\rm s}
when we
place either description in the
\frac{1}{2}\Omega
-background, each with its own effective
twisted superpotential
\widetilde{\mathcal{W}}^{\text{eff}}
.

This extra data may be encoded in a half-BPS boundary condition at infinity in
\mathbb{R}_{\epsilon}^{2}
. This is the boundary condition that defines the second Lagrangian submanifold in the NRS conjecture. A full classification and analysis of such boundary conditions is likely to be quite rich and has not been completed thus far (to our knowledge), however see
[
NW10
,
DGV15
,
CCV11
]
for various examples and related discussions. In particular, some simple choices have been studied in
[
NW10
, Section 3.4]
. Especially relevant here is the type I (or standard Neumann) boundary condition, which is characterized in a four-dimensional
gauge
theory by Neumann boundary conditions for the components of the gauge fields parallel to the boundary, and Dirichlet boundary conditions for the component of the gauge field normal to the boundary. For an abelian gauge theory this would correspond to a Lagrangian submanifold in the Hitchin moduli space defined by the condition
\exp(\oint_{B}\,\lambda/\epsilon)=1
. For a non-abelian gauge theory, with a given choice of an electric-magnetic duality frame, this Lagrangian submanifold would be
approximated
by the condition

\displaystyle\exp\Big(\frac{1}{\epsilon}\,\oint_{B_{i}}\,\lambda\Big)=1\ ,

(5.5)

for all
i=1,\dots,\frac{1}{2}\,h_{1}(\overline{\Sigma})
, near infinity on the Coulomb branch.

Here, expanding on
[
HN20
]
, we want to speculate more generally, for any
{\mathcal{N}}=2
theory
{\sf T}={\sf T}_{K}[C,\mathcal{D}]
of class
\mathcal{S}
, that the relevant class of half-BPS boundary conditions are labelled in the infrared by a distinguished spectral network
\mathscr{W}=\mathscr{W}(u,\vartheta_{\rm c})
together with a polarization (a choice of
A
-cycles and
B
-cycles) of the Seiberg-Witten curve
\Sigma_{u}
. The spectral network
\mathscr{W}
, together with the electric-magnetic splitting, determine a choice of holomorphic exponentiated Darboux coordinates
\mathcal{X}_{\gamma}
on the moduli space of flat
\epsilon
-connections
\mathscr{M}_{\rm flat}^{\epsilon}(C,SL(K,\mathbb{C}))
(with respect to the complex structure
J_{\epsilon}
). In terms of these Darboux coordinates, the second Lagrangian submanifold in the NRS conjecture is given
exactly
by

\displaystyle\mathcal{X}_{B_{i}}=1

(5.6)

for all
i=1,\dots,\frac{1}{2}\,h_{1}(\overline{\Sigma})
.
35
35

35

This proposal is somewhat similar in flavour to the boundary conditions described in
[
DGV15
,
CCV11
]
.

For example, any theory
\sf T
with a weakly coupled Lagrangian description (such as the pure
SU(2)
theory at weak coupling) comes with a canonical choice of
A
-cycles
A^{i}
and
B
-cycles
B_{i}
on the Seiberg-Witten curve

\Sigma_{u}
: in a limit where an exponentiated gauge coupling
q_{i}
tends to zero,
\exp(\oint_{A^{i}}\,\lambda)
becomes exponentially small, whereas
\exp(\oint_{B_{i}}\,\lambda)
becomes exponentially large. Furthermore, there is a critical phase

\vartheta_{\rm c}
in a neighbourhood of this weak coupling point for which the
A
-cycle period satisfies
{\mathrm{e}}^{-{\mathrm{i}}\,\vartheta_{\rm c}}\,\oint_{A^{i}}\,\lambda<0
, and the corresponding spectral network

\mathscr{W}^{\textrm{\tiny FN}}=\mathscr{W}(u,\vartheta_{\rm c})
is a Fenchel-Nielsen type network.
36
36

36

Whenever the description of the theory
\sf T
includes non-zero mass parameters, the isotopy class of the Fenchel-Nielsen type network depends on the values of these parameters (or better, on the wall-crossing chamber these values are part of), and is thus not quite unique. We believe these differences play no significant role in the following. See
[
CPT20
]
for a related discussion in five dimensions where these jumps are related to flop transitions.
The
closed trajectories of this network correspond to an infinite tower
of BPS particles, including the
W^{\pm}
-bosons, accumulating at this
phase.

On the other hand, suppose that
\sf T
is an asymptotically free theory near a strong coupling point with a dual weakly coupled description. For example, say
\sf T
is the pure
SU(2)
theory at strong coupling.
37
37

37

This example will be treated in detail in a forthcoming paper.
Then the distinguished spectral network
\mathscr{W}(u,\vartheta_{\rm c})
would be a critical Fock-Goncharov type network, with a saddle signalling the presence of a massless dyon. In this case, the massless dyon determines the
A
-cycle in the polarization of the Seiberg-Witten curve
\Sigma_{u}
.
38
38

38

One might worry that there is no unique such critical Fock-Goncharov type network nor choice of
B
-cycle, but these ambiguities just illustrate the
SL(2,\mathbb{Z})
monodromy around the strong coupling point.

Finally, suppose that
\sf T
is an intrinsically strongly coupled theory. For example, say
\sf T
is the
E_{6}
Minahan-Nemeschansky theory. In this case we propose that the different boundary conditions are labelled by the higher rank Fenchel-Nielsen type spectral networks
\mathscr{W}(u,\vartheta_{\rm c})
where the critical phase
\vartheta_{\rm c}
satisfies
{\mathrm{e}}^{-{\mathrm{i}}\,\vartheta_{\rm c}}\,\oint_{\gamma}\,\lambda<0
for a one-cycle
\gamma
on the Seiberg-Witten curve
\Sigma_{u}
. This one-cycle also determines the polarization of
\Sigma_{u}
.

With all this in mind, we now turn the NRS conjecture into a concrete geometric recipe for computing the effective twisted superpotential
\widetilde{\mathcal{W}}^{\text{eff}}
for any
{\mathcal{N}}=2
theory
{\sf T}={\sf T}_{K}[C,\mathcal{D}]
of class
\mathcal{S}
in the
\frac{1}{2}\Omega
-background, with respect to a chosen half-BPS boundary condition at infinity in the
\mathbb{R}^{2}_{\epsilon}
plane, that is characterized by a spectral network
\mathscr{W}
as well as a polarization of the Seiberg-Witten curve
\Sigma_{u}
. Following
[
HK18
]
, this recipe reads:

1.

Write down the brane of
\epsilon
-opers
\mathscr{L}_{\epsilon}^{\rm oper}

associated to the theory
\sf T
(using the explanations of
Section
4.3
).

2.

Compute the “length” and “twist” coordinates

\displaystyle x_{i}=\frac{\epsilon}{\pi\,{\mathrm{i}}}\log(-\mathcal{X}_{A^{i}})\qquad\mbox{and}\qquad y^{i}=\frac{1}{2\,\epsilon}\log\mathcal{X}_{B_{i}}

(5.7)

associated to the spectral network

\mathscr{W}
(following the instructions from
Section
2
).

3.

Evaluate the coordinates
(x_{i},y^{i})
on the brane of

\epsilon
-opers
\mathscr{L}_{\epsilon}^{\rm oper}
, with respect to the WKB-framing
(as discussed in Section
4.4
).
39
39

39

Recall that there may be multiple
\mathscr{W}
-abelianizations, corresponding to distinct
\mathscr{W}
-framings.

4.

Finally, extract
\widetilde{\mathcal{W}}^{\text{eff}}
through

\displaystyle y^{i}=\frac{1}{\epsilon}\,\frac{\partial\widetilde{\mathcal{W}}^{\text{eff}}(x;\epsilon)}{\partial x_{i}}\ .

(5.8)

This recipe computes the effective twisted superpotential
\widetilde{\mathcal{W}}^{\text{eff}}
exactly in the parameter
\epsilon
. As explained in Section
4.4
, the spectral coordinates
\mathcal{X}_{\gamma}
, evaluated on the brane of
\epsilon
-opers, may be interpreted as non-perturbative completions of the quantum periods
\Pi_{\gamma}(\epsilon)=\frac{1}{\epsilon}\,\big(\oint_{\gamma}\,\lambda+O(\epsilon)\big)
.
40
40

40

More precisely,
\log\mathcal{X}_{\gamma}(\nabla_{\epsilon}^{\rm oper})
is an analytic continuation of the Voros symbol
V_{\gamma}(\epsilon)
computed at
{\rm arg}(\epsilon)=\vartheta
. If
\vartheta=\vartheta_{\rm c}
is a critical phase, it is an analytic continuation of the average of the Voros symbols computed at
{\rm arg}(\epsilon)=\vartheta_{\rm c}^{\pm}
.
The
\epsilon
-expansion for
\widetilde{\mathcal{W}}^{\text{eff}}
may then be computed from the equations
\Pi_{B_{i}}(\epsilon)=2\,\partial_{x_{i}}\widetilde{\mathcal{W}}^{\text{eff}}(x;\epsilon)
, where for
x_{i}
we substitute the quantum period
\epsilon\,\Pi_{A^{i}}(\epsilon)/\pi\,{\mathrm{i}}
.

For theories
\sf T
with a weakly coupled Lagrangian formulation, this recipe computes the well-known Nekrasov-Shatashvili limit of the Nekrasov partition function with respect to the distinguished Fenchel-Nielsen type network
\mathscr{W}^{\textrm{\tiny FN}}
described above. The coordinate
x_{i}
then corresponds to a complexified Fenchel-Nielsen length parameter, which we will denote in the following by
-a_{\textrm{\tiny FN},i}
since it may be obtained by Borel resummation of the quantum period
\Pi_{A^{i}}(\epsilon)
with respect to the Fenchel-Nielsen type network
\mathscr{W}^{\textrm{\tiny FN}}
.
This was already tested in
[
HK18
]
for the weakly coupled conformal
SU(2)
and
SU(3)
examples. Let us take a look at some new illustrative examples here, starting with the very simplest model of a theory of class
\mathcal{S}
.

5.3
AD
2
Theory

Let us compute
\widetilde{\mathcal{W}}^{\text{eff}}
for the Argyres-Douglas theory AD
2
following
the geometric recipe. Recall that this is the four-dimensional
{\mathcal{N}}=2

field theory of a single free hypermultiplet of mass
m
,
corresponding to the Seiberg-Witten curve

\displaystyle\Sigma\colon\quad w^{2}=z^{2}+m\ ,

(5.9)

viewed as a branched covering
\Sigma\to C
of degree two over

C=\mathbb{C}
. This geometry is illustrated in
Figure
31
. Although the AD
2
Lagrangian has merely a
free hypermultiplet contribution, we can introduce gauge degrees of
freedom by gauging the flavour symmetry of hypermultiplet. As we saw
earlier, this turns
m
into a variable parameterizing a complex
one-dimensional Coulomb branch
\mathscr{B}
. The corresponding
A
-cycle
and
B
-cycle are illustrated in Figure
39
, together with the relevant
critical network
\mathscr{W}
(which we already
encountered in Example
1
, together with its resolutions

\mathscr{W}^{\pm}
in Example
7
).

\begin{overpic}[AD2FNCycles.png]
\put(71.0,54.0){$s_{1}$}
\put(27.0,54.0){$s_{2}$}
\put(27.0,1.0){$s_{3}$}
\put(71.0,1.0){$s_{4}$}
\put(33.0,48.0){$12$}
\put(63.0,48.0){$21$}
\put(33.0,7.0){$21$}
\put(63.0,7.0){$12$}
\put(45.0,50.0){{\color[rgb]{0.1953,0.1953,1}$(s_{1},s_{2})$}}
\put(45.0,5.0){{\color[rgb]{0.1953,0.1953,1}$(s_{3},s_{4})$}}
\put(32.0,27.0){{\color[rgb]{0.1953,0.1953,1}$(s_{3},s_{2})$}}
\put(59.0,27.0){{\color[rgb]{0.1953,0.1953,1}$(s_{1},s_{4})$}}
\put(42.0,31.0){{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}$A$}}
\put(31.0,38.0){{\color[rgb]{0.1953,0.1953,1}$B$}}
\end{overpic}

Figure 39:
Critical network
\mathscr{W}
for the AD
2
theory together with a choice of
A
-cycle and
B
-cycle as well as framing data
s_{1},s_{2},s_{3},s_{4}
.

The associated complex two-dimensional moduli space
\mathscr{M}_{\rm flat}^{\epsilon}(C,SL(2,\mathbb{C}))
of flat
\mathscr{W}
-framed

\epsilon
-connections
\nabla_{\epsilon}
was described in
Example
7
, while the corresponding length and twist coordinates

\displaystyle x=\frac{\epsilon}{\pi\,{\mathrm{i}}}\log(-\mathcal{X}_{A})\qquad\mbox{and}\qquad y^{\pm}=\frac{1}{2\,\epsilon}\log\mathcal{X}_{B}^{\pm}

(5.10)

were constructed in Example
10
. Recall
that the framing of
\nabla_{\epsilon}
(which is the same for the
network
\mathscr{W}
and its resolutions
\mathscr{W}^{\pm}
) consists of
a local section
s_{l}
at each of the marked points
z_{l}
at infinity
(see Figure
39
), and that the spectral coordinates

\displaystyle\mathcal{X}_{A}=\frac{s_{1}\wedge s_{2}}{s_{3}\wedge s_{2}}\,\frac{s_{3}\wedge s_{4}}{s_{1}\wedge s_{4}}\ ,\quad\mathcal{X}_{B}^{+}=\frac{s_{1}\wedge s_{4}}{s_{2}\wedge s_{4}}\qquad\mbox{and}\qquad\mathcal{X}_{B}^{-}=-\frac{s_{1}\wedge s_{3}}{s_{1}\wedge s_{2}}

(5.11)

are determined in terms of this framing data.

The brane of
\epsilon
-opers
\mathscr{L}_{\epsilon}^{\rm oper}
was found
to be parameterized by the Schrödinger equation

\displaystyle{\rm D}_{\epsilon}\psi(z)=\epsilon^{2}\,\psi^{\prime\prime}(z)-(z^{2}+m)\,\psi(z)=0

(5.12)

in Example
19
. As we saw in
Example
24
, when
{\rm arg}(\epsilon)=\pi/2
and

m>0
the spectral network
\mathscr{W}
matches with the Stokes
graph for the Schrödinger oper
{\rm D}_{\epsilon}
. In that case the
spectral coordinates
\mathcal{X}_{A}
and
\mathcal{X}_{B}^{\pm}
may be computed as Voros
symbols using exact WKB analysis.

Here we let
m
and
\epsilon
be
free
variables,
and evaluate
\mathcal{X}_{A}
and
\mathcal{X}_{B}^{\pm}
on the complete family of
opers
{\rm D}_{\epsilon}
. We frame the flat
\epsilon
-oper connection

\nabla_{\epsilon}^{\rm oper}
by choosing

\displaystyle s_{l}(z)=\begin{pmatrix}-\epsilon\,\psi_{l}^{\prime}(z)\\
\psi_{l}(z)\end{pmatrix}

(5.13)

as in Example
19
. That is, the abelianization of

\nabla_{\epsilon}^{\rm oper}
is described in terms of the parabolic
cylinder functions

\displaystyle\psi_{1}(z)

\displaystyle=D_{\mu}\Big(\sqrt{\frac{2}{\epsilon}}\,z\Big)\ ,

\displaystyle\psi_{2}(z)

\displaystyle={\mathrm{e}}^{-\frac{\pi\,{\mathrm{i}}}{2}\,(\mu+1)}\,D_{-\mu-1}\Big(-\sqrt{\frac{2}{\epsilon}}\,{\mathrm{i}}\,z\Big)\ ,

\displaystyle\psi_{3}(z)

\displaystyle={\mathrm{e}}^{\,\pi\,{\mathrm{i}}\,\mu}\,D_{\mu}\Big(-\sqrt{\frac{2}{\epsilon}}\,z\Big)\ ,

\displaystyle\psi_{4}(z)

\displaystyle={\mathrm{e}}^{-\frac{\pi\,{\mathrm{i}}}{2}\,(3\,\mu-1)}\,D_{-\mu-1}\Big(\sqrt{\frac{2}{\epsilon}}\,{\mathrm{i}}\,z\Big)\ ,

(5.14)

with
\mu=-\frac{m}{2\,\epsilon}-\frac{1}{2}
. This is the framing provided by
the exact WKB method when

m>0
and
{\rm arg}(\epsilon)=\frac{\pi}{2}
, but now analytically
continued for all choices of
m
and
\epsilon
.
Since the exterior product of these sections is given by the Wronskian

\displaystyle s_{k}\wedge s_{l}=\epsilon\,\text{Wr}(\psi_{k},\psi_{l})\ ,

(5.15)

we find

\displaystyle\mathcal{X}_{A}(\nabla_{\epsilon}^{\rm oper})

\displaystyle=-\exp\Big(-\pi\,{\mathrm{i}}\,\frac{m}{\epsilon}\Big)\ ,

\displaystyle\mathcal{X}_{B}^{+}(\nabla_{\epsilon}^{\rm oper})

\displaystyle=\frac{{\mathrm{i}}}{\sqrt{2\pi}}\,\Gamma\Big(\frac{1}{2}-\frac{m}{2\,\epsilon}\Big)\ ,

\displaystyle\mathcal{X}_{B}^{-}(\nabla_{\epsilon}^{\rm oper})

\displaystyle=\frac{{\mathrm{i}}\,\sqrt{2\pi}\,{\mathrm{e}}^{-\pi\,{\mathrm{i}}\,\frac{m}{2\,\epsilon}}}{\Gamma\big(\frac{1}{2}+\frac{m}{2\,\epsilon}\big)}\ .

(5.16)

Finally, we now have all the ingredients we need to compute

\widetilde{\mathcal{W}}^{\text{eff}}
. We define the length and twist coordinates
x
and
y
by averaging
over the coordinates in the two resolutions (similarly
to
[
HK18
, Section 10]
) to get

\displaystyle x

\displaystyle=\frac{\epsilon}{\pi\,{\mathrm{i}}}\log(-\mathcal{X}_{A})=-m\ ,

\displaystyle y

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{2\,\epsilon}\log\sqrt{\mathcal{X}_{B}^{+}\,\mathcal{X}_{B}^{-}}=\frac{1}{4\,\epsilon}\log\frac{\Gamma\big(\frac{1}{2}+\frac{x}{2\,\epsilon}\big)}{\Gamma\big(\frac{1}{2}-\frac{x}{2\,\epsilon}\big)}\ ,

(5.17)

where we dropped a linear term
\frac{1}{4\,\epsilon^{2}}\,x
from
y
.
41
41

41

That is,
we fix the shift ambiguity in the twist coordinate by defining
y

as in (
5.17
).
The effective twisted superpotential

\widetilde{\mathcal{W}}^{\text{eff}}
is then obtained by integrating the relation

\displaystyle y=\frac{1}{\epsilon}\,\frac{\partial\widetilde{\mathcal{W}}^{\text{eff}}(x;\epsilon)}{\partial x}\ .

(5.18)

Recall the special function from Section
4.1
given by

\displaystyle\Upsilon(x)=\int_{\frac{1}{2}}^{x}\,\log\frac{\Gamma(x^{\prime})}{\Gamma(1-x^{\prime})}\,\mathrm{d}x^{\prime}\ ,

(5.19)

with the properties

\displaystyle\frac{\partial}{\partial x}\Upsilon(a+b\,x)=b\log\frac{\Gamma(a+b\,x)}{\Gamma(1-a-b\,x)}\qquad\mbox{and}\qquad\Upsilon(1-x)=\Upsilon(x)\ ,

(5.20)

for constants
a,b
.
It then follows that

\widetilde{\mathcal{W}}^{\text{eff}}(x;\epsilon)=\frac{\epsilon}{2}\,\Upsilon\Big(\frac{1}{2}+\frac{x}{2\,\epsilon}\Big)=\frac{\epsilon}{2}\,\Upsilon\Big(\frac{1}{2}+\frac{m}{2\,\epsilon}\Big)\ ,

(5.21)

up to an integration constant that is independent of
x
. This indeed agrees
with the effective twisted superpotential
\widetilde{\mathcal{W}}^{\text{eff}}(m;\epsilon)
from
(
4.11
).

5.4
Pure
SU(2)
Theory

As our second example, we compute the superpotential
\widetilde{\mathcal{W}}^{\text{eff}}
for the weakly coupled pure
SU(2)
theory described in Section
3.1
. Recall that the Seiberg-Witten curve corresponding to the pure
SU(2)
theory is a ramified double covering
\Sigma\to C
over the twice-punctured sphere, given by the equation

\displaystyle w^{2}=\frac{\Lambda^{2}}{z^{3}}-\frac{2\,u}{z^{2}}+\frac{\Lambda^{2}}{z}\ .

(5.22)

This geometry was illustrated in Figure
21
. In (
5.22
),
u
is a parameter on the complex one-dimensional Coulomb branch and
\Lambda
is the ultraviolet scale. Since we consider the weakly coupled pure
SU(2)
theory, we assume
|\Lambda^{2}/u|\ll 1
.

In Example
11
we constructed the relevant Fenchel-Nielsen coordinates on the corresponding complex two-dimensional moduli space
\mathscr{M}_{\rm flat}^{\epsilon}(C,SL(2,\mathbb{C}))
of framed flat
\epsilon
-connections with respect to the spectral network and its resolutions, see Figure
15
.
42
42

42

Although this network was drawn for specific values of
\Lambda
and
u
, an isotopic network may be found at any point in the weak coupling region.
For convenience, we have drawn this network again in Figure
40
.

\begin{overpic}[PureSU2FNCycles.png]
\put(7.0,38.0){$21$}
\put(22.0,50.0){$12$}
\put(22.0,19.0){$21$}
\put(48.0,50.0){$12$}
\put(48.0,19.0){$21$}
\put(47.0,38.0){$21$}
\put(20.0,35.0){{\color[rgb]{0.1953,0.1953,1}$\left(s_{1}^{\prime\prime},s_{2}^{\prime\prime}\right)$}}
\put(53.0,24.0){{\color[rgb]{0.1953,0.1953,1}$\left(s_{1},s_{2}\right)$}}
\put(50.0,42.0){{\color[rgb]{0.1953,0.1953,1}$\left({\sf M}s_{1},{\sf M}s_{2}\right)$}}
\put(5.0,26.0){{\color[rgb]{0.1953,0.1953,1}$\left(s_{1}^{\prime},s_{2}^{\prime}\right)$}}
\put(27.0,42.0){{\color[rgb]{0.1953,0.1953,1}$B$}}
\put(31.0,24.0){{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}$A$}}
\put(34.0,35.0){{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}$1$}}
\put(15.0,40.0){{\color[rgb]{0.1953,0.1953,1}$1$}}
\put(15.0,28.0){{\color[rgb]{0.1953,0.1953,1}$2$}}
\end{overpic}

Figure 40:
Fenchel-Nielsen type network for the pure
SU(2)
theory in the weak coupling regime together with a choice of
A
-cycle and
B
-cycle, drawn at
u=-\frac{9}{8}
and
\vartheta=\frac{\pi}{2}
.

Recall that the framing data consists of a choice of local sections
s_{1}\in\ell
,
s_{1}^{\prime}\in\ell^{\prime}
and
s_{2}^{\prime\prime}\in\ell^{\prime\prime}
, where
\ell
and
\ell^{\prime}
are
\nabla
-invariant line bundles in a neighbourhood of
z=0
and
z=\infty
, respectively, and
\ell^{\prime\prime}
is an eigenline of the counterclockwise monodromy
\sf M
of
\nabla
(see Example
8
).

In terms of these choices, we found in Example
11
that the spectral coordinate
\mathcal{X}_{A}
is the eigenvalue of
\sf M
corresponding to the eigenvector
s_{2}^{\prime\prime}
, while the
B
-cycle coordinates are

\displaystyle\mathcal{X}_{B}^{-}

\displaystyle=\mathcal{X}_{A}^{2}\,\frac{(s_{1}\wedge s_{2}^{\prime\prime})^{2}\,(s_{1}^{\prime}\wedge s_{1}^{\prime\prime})^{2}}{(s_{1}\wedge s_{2})\,(s_{1}^{\prime}\wedge s_{2}^{\prime})\,(s_{1}^{\prime\prime}\wedge s_{2}^{\prime\prime})^{2}}\ ,

\displaystyle\mathcal{X}_{B}^{+}

\displaystyle=\mathcal{X}_{A}^{-2}\,\frac{(s_{1}\wedge s_{2})\,(s_{1}^{\prime}\wedge s_{2}^{\prime})\,(s_{1}^{\prime\prime}\wedge s_{2}^{\prime\prime})^{2}}{(s_{1}\wedge s_{1}^{\prime\prime})^{2}\,(s_{1}^{\prime}\wedge s_{2}^{\prime\prime})^{2}}\ ,

\displaystyle\mathcal{X}_{B}

\displaystyle=\sqrt{\mathcal{X}_{B}^{-}\,\mathcal{X}_{B}^{+}}=\frac{s_{1}^{\prime}\wedge s_{1}^{\prime\prime}}{s_{1}\wedge s_{1}^{\prime\prime}}\,\frac{s_{1}\wedge s_{2}^{\prime\prime}}{s_{1}^{\prime}\wedge s_{2}^{\prime\prime}}\ .

(5.23)

Bear in mind that the sections
s_{1}
,
s_{1}^{\prime}
and
s_{2}^{\prime\prime}
should be evaluated at the same point
z\in C
.

The brane of
\epsilon
-opers
\mathscr{L}^{\rm oper}_{\epsilon}
is parametrized by the Mathieu equation

\displaystyle{\rm D}_{\epsilon}\psi(z)=\epsilon^{2}\,\frac{\partial^{2}\psi(z)}{\partial z^{2}}-\Big(\frac{\Lambda^{2}}{z^{3}}-\frac{2\,u+\epsilon^{2}/4}{z^{2}}+\frac{\Lambda^{2}}{z}\Big)\,\psi(z)=0\ ,

(5.24)

for
|u|\gg\Lambda^{2}
, as found in Example
20
. As in Section
5.3
, we let
\Lambda
,
u
and
\epsilon
be free variables with
|u/\Lambda^{2}|\gg 1
, and evaluate
\mathcal{X}_{A}
and
\mathcal{X}_{B}
on the whole family of opers
{\rm D}_{\epsilon}
. The local sections
s(z)
are related to the solutions
\psi(z)
of (
5.24
) as

\displaystyle s(z)=\bigg(\begin{matrix}-\epsilon\,\frac{\partial\psi(z)}{\partial z}\\
\psi(z)\end{matrix}\bigg)\ .

(5.25)

We fix the framing data by letting
\psi_{1}(z,\Lambda)
be the asymptotically small solution of (
5.24
) at
z=0
(when approaching
z=0
along the negative real axis), while similarly
\psi_{1}^{\prime}(z,\Lambda)
is the asymptotically small solution at
z=\infty
(when approaching
z=\infty
along the negative real axis) and
\psi_{2}^{\prime\prime}(z,\Lambda)
is the small eigenvector of the monodromy
\sf M
of
\nabla_{\epsilon}^{\rm oper}
, all for
-u\gg\Lambda^{2}
and
{\rm arg}(\epsilon)=\pi/2
. This is the framing provided by the exact WKB method for these choices of parameters, but now analytically continued to all
u,\epsilon\in\mathbb{C}
.

Unlike in Section
5.3
, the functions
\psi_{1}(z,\Lambda)
,
\psi_{1}^{\prime}(z,\Lambda)
and
\psi_{2}^{\prime\prime}(z,\Lambda)
are not known in exact form. But it is possible to find them as series expansions in
\Lambda^{2}
, while exact in
\epsilon
. For this, we follow the approach of
[
HK18
, Section 9]
.

We start with the eigenfunctions
\psi_{i}^{\prime\prime}(z,\Lambda)
of the monodromy
\sf M
. Note that the Mathieu equation (
5.24
) is parametrized in terms of
\Lambda^{2}
. Since
\Lambda^{2}\ll|u|
, we expand

\displaystyle u=\sum_{k=0}^{\infty}\,u_{k}\,\Lambda^{2k}

(5.26)

and

\displaystyle\psi_{i}^{\prime\prime}(z,\Lambda)=\sum_{k=0}^{\infty}\,f_{k,i}^{\prime\prime}(z)\,\Lambda^{2k}\ .

(5.27)

At order
\Lambda^{0}
, the Mathieu equation (
5.24
) reads

\displaystyle\epsilon^{2}\,\frac{\partial^{2}f_{0}^{\prime\prime}(z)}{\partial z^{2}}+\frac{8\,u_{0}+\epsilon^{2}}{4\,z^{2}}\,f_{0}^{\prime\prime}(z)=0\ ,

(5.28)

with the two independent solutions

\displaystyle f_{0,i}^{\prime\prime}(z)=\alpha_{i}\,z^{\frac{1}{2}\pm\frac{a_{\textrm{\tiny FN}}}{2\,\epsilon}}

(5.29)

if we choose
u_{0}=-a_{\textrm{\tiny FN}}^{2}/8
. These solutions satisfy
f_{0,i}^{\prime\prime}({\mathrm{e}}^{\,2\pi\,{\mathrm{i}}}\,z)=-{\mathrm{e}}^{\pm\,\pi\,{\mathrm{i}}\,a_{\textrm{\tiny FN}}/\epsilon}\,f_{0,i}^{\prime\prime}(z)
; since we are looking for functions
\psi_{i}^{\prime\prime}(z,\Lambda)
that diagonalize the monodromy around
z=0
with eigenvalue
-{\mathrm{e}}^{\pm\,\pi\,{\mathrm{i}}\,a_{\textrm{\tiny FN}}/\epsilon}
, this is the property we require for all
f_{k,i}^{\prime\prime}(z)
. Note that if we choose
a_{\textrm{\tiny FN}}>0
, then
\psi_{2}^{\prime\prime}(z,\Lambda)
is indeed the small eigenvector for
-u\gg\Lambda^{2}
and
{\rm arg}(\epsilon)=\frac{\pi}{2}
.

This guides us to the ansatz

\displaystyle f_{k,i}^{\prime\prime}(z)=\sum_{m=-k}^{k}\,c_{k,\pm}^{(m)}\,z^{\frac{1}{2}\pm\frac{a_{\textrm{\tiny FN}}}{2\,\epsilon}+m}\ .

(5.30)

This ansatz indeed solves the Mathieu equation (
5.24
) at order
\Lambda^{2}
, which reads

\displaystyle\epsilon^{2}\,\frac{\partial^{2}f_{1}^{\prime\prime}(z)}{\partial z^{2}}+\frac{8\,u_{0}+\epsilon^{2}}{4\,z^{2}}\,f_{1}^{\prime\prime}(z)-\Big(\frac{1}{z^{3}}-\frac{2\,u_{1}}{z^{2}}+\frac{1}{z}\Big)\,f_{0}^{\prime\prime}(z)=0\ ,

(5.31)

if we choose

\displaystyle c_{1,\pm}^{(-1)}=\pm\,\frac{1}{\epsilon\,(a_{\textrm{\tiny FN}}\pm\epsilon)}\ ,\quad u_{1}=0\qquad\mbox{and}\qquad c_{1,\pm}^{(1)}=\mp\,\frac{1}{\epsilon\,(a_{\textrm{\tiny FN}}\mp\epsilon)}\ ,

(5.32)

while
c_{1,\pm}^{(0)}
is unconstrained. The same strategy can be applied to go to any order in
\Lambda^{2}
. For instance, at order
\Lambda^{4}
we find

\displaystyle c_{2,\pm}^{(-1)}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{2\,\epsilon^{2}\,(a_{\textrm{\tiny FN}}\pm 2\,\epsilon)\,(a_{\textrm{\tiny FN}}\pm\epsilon)}\ ,\quad c_{2,\pm}^{(-1)}=\pm\,\frac{c_{1,\pm}^{(0)}}{\epsilon\,(a_{\textrm{\tiny FN}}\pm\epsilon)}\ ,\quad u_{2}=-\frac{1}{a_{\textrm{\tiny FN}}^{2}-\epsilon^{2}}\ ,

\displaystyle c_{2,\pm}^{(1)}

\displaystyle=\mp\,\frac{c_{1,\pm}^{(0)}}{\epsilon\,(a_{\textrm{\tiny FN}}\mp\epsilon)}\qquad\mbox{and}\qquad c_{2,\pm}^{(2)}=-\frac{1}{2\,\epsilon^{2}\,(a_{\textrm{\tiny FN}}\mp 2\,\epsilon)\,(a_{\textrm{\tiny FN}}\mp\epsilon)}\ ,

(5.33)

while
c_{2,\pm}^{(0)}
is again undetermined, and so on.

Computing the asymptotically small solution
\psi_{1}(z,\Lambda)
near
z=0
is a little trickier. This requires explicit knowledge of the expansion

\displaystyle u=-\frac{a_{\textrm{\tiny FN}}^{2}}{8}-\frac{\Lambda^{4}}{a_{\textrm{\tiny FN}}^{2}-\epsilon^{2}}-\frac{5\,a_{\textrm{\tiny FN}}^{2}+7\,\epsilon^{2}}{(a_{\textrm{\tiny FN}}^{2}-4\,\epsilon^{2})\,(a_{\textrm{\tiny FN}}^{2}-\epsilon^{2})^{3}}\,\Lambda^{8}+O(\Lambda^{10})

(5.34)

which we just computed. We introduce a new coordinate
v=z/\Lambda^{2}
which is of order
1
even if
z
is very small. In this coordinate the Mathieu equation (
5.24
) reads

\displaystyle\epsilon^{2}\,\frac{\partial^{2}g(v)}{\partial v^{2}}-\Big(\frac{1}{v^{3}}-\frac{8\,u+\epsilon^{2}}{4\,v^{2}}+\frac{\Lambda^{4}}{v}\Big)\,g(v)=0\ ,

(5.35)

with
g(v):=\psi(\Lambda^{2}\,v)
. As before, we expand
g(v)
, this time as a series in
\Lambda^{4}
:

\displaystyle g(v)=\sum_{l=0}^{\infty}\,g_{l}(v)\,\Lambda^{4l}\ .

(5.36)

At order
\Lambda^{0}
, the rescaled Mathieu equation (
5.35
) reads

\displaystyle\epsilon^{2}\,\frac{\partial^{2}g_{0}(v)}{\partial v^{2}}-\Big(\frac{1}{v^{3}}-\frac{8\,u_{0}+\epsilon^{2}}{4\,v^{2}}\Big)\,g_{0}(v)=0\ .

(5.37)

For the two independent solutions
g_{0,i}(v)
we choose the Hankel functions

\displaystyle g_{0,i}(v)=\sqrt{v}\ H^{([i+1])}\Big(\frac{a_{\textrm{\tiny FN}}}{\epsilon},\frac{2\,{\mathrm{i}}}{\epsilon\,\sqrt{v}}\Big)\ ,

(5.38)

where the index
[i+1]
is understood modulo
2
, because they have the correct asymptotics

\displaystyle H^{(i)}\Big(\frac{a_{\textrm{\tiny FN}}}{\epsilon},\frac{2\,{\mathrm{i}}}{\epsilon\,\sqrt{v}}\Big)\simeq\sqrt{-{\mathrm{i}}\,\epsilon}\ v^{1/4}\,{\mathrm{e}}^{\mp\,2/\epsilon\,\sqrt{v}}

(5.39)

when
v\to 0
with
-\pi<{\rm arg}(2\,{\mathrm{i}}/\epsilon\,\sqrt{v}\,)<2\pi
;
43
43

43

Note that the Hankel functions are multivalued functions, and so are really defined on the universal cover of
\mathbb{C}\setminus\{0\}
.
note that
H^{(1)}
has exponent
-\frac{2}{\epsilon\,\sqrt{v}}<0
when
{\rm arg}(\epsilon)=\frac{\pi}{2}
and
v
has negative real part as well as negative imaginary part. It is also useful to know
[
AS65
, Equation 9.1.39]

\displaystyle H^{(2)}(\alpha,{\mathrm{e}}^{-\pi\,{\mathrm{i}}}\,z)=-{\mathrm{e}}^{-\pi\,{\mathrm{i}}\,\alpha}\,H^{(1)}(\alpha,z)\ ,

(5.40)

which implies

\displaystyle g_{0,1}({\mathrm{e}}^{\,2\pi\,{\mathrm{i}}}\,v)={\mathrm{e}}^{-\pi\,{\mathrm{i}}\,a_{\textrm{\tiny FN}}/\epsilon}\,g_{0,2}(v)\ .

(5.41)

Since the solutions
\psi_{1}(z,\Lambda)
and
\psi_{2}(z,\Lambda)
near
z=0
should be related as
{\sf M}\psi_{1}(z,\Lambda)=\psi_{2}(z,\Lambda)
, this equation implies that the leading order of the solution
\psi_{i}(z,\Lambda)
needs to be

\displaystyle\psi_{i}(z,\Lambda)={\mathrm{e}}^{\pm\,\pi\,{\mathrm{i}}\,a_{\textrm{\tiny FN}}/2\,\epsilon}\,g_{0,i}(v)+O(\Lambda^{4})\ .

(5.42)

At order
\Lambda^{4}
, the equation (
5.35
) reads

\displaystyle\epsilon^{2}\,\frac{\partial^{2}g_{1}(v)}{\partial v^{2}}-\Big(\frac{1}{v^{3}}+\frac{a_{\textrm{\tiny FN}}^{2}+\epsilon^{2}}{v^{2}}\Big)\,g_{1}(v)-\Big(\frac{1}{v}+\frac{2}{(a_{\textrm{\tiny FN}}^{2}-\epsilon^{2})\,v^{2}}\Big)\,g_{0}(v)=0\ .

(5.43)

Since we are looking for solutions
g(v)
with the asymptotics (
5.39
) when
v\to 0
, we propose the ansatz

\displaystyle g_{1,i}(v)=\sum_{n=\pm\,1}\,d_{1,i}^{(n)}\,\sqrt{v}\ H^{([i+1])}\Big(\frac{a_{\textrm{\tiny FN}}}{\epsilon}+2\,n,\frac{2\,{\mathrm{i}}}{\epsilon\,\sqrt{v}}\Big)\ .

(5.44)

This indeed solves (
5.43
) when

\displaystyle d_{1,i}^{(-1)}=\mp\,\frac{1}{a_{\textrm{\tiny FN}}\,\epsilon\,(a_{\textrm{\tiny FN}}\pm\epsilon)^{2}}\qquad\mbox{and}\qquad d_{1,i}^{(+1)}=\pm\,\frac{1}{a_{\textrm{\tiny FN}}\,\epsilon\,(a_{\textrm{\tiny FN}}\mp\epsilon)^{2}}\ .

(5.45)

We can extend this analysis to higher orders in
\Lambda^{4}
by making the ansatz

\displaystyle g_{l,i}(v)=\sum_{n=-l}^{l}\,d_{l,i}^{(n)}\,\sqrt{v}\ H^{([i+1])}\Big(\frac{a_{\textrm{\tiny FN}}}{\epsilon}+2\,n,\frac{2\,{\mathrm{i}}}{\epsilon\,\sqrt{v}}\Big)\ ,

(5.46)

and solving (
5.35
) at order
\Lambda^{4l}
for
d_{l,i}^{(n)}
.

We are not done yet though, as we are looking for solutions
\psi(z)
of the original Mathieu equation (
5.24
) with the correct asymptotics as
z\to 0
, in a series expansion in
\Lambda^{2}
. Thus we need to analytically continue the functions
g_{i}(v)
to
v\to\infty
, while keeping
z=\Lambda^{2}\,v
of order
1
. For this, we use the fact that the Hankel functions
H^{(i)}
have the series expansions

\displaystyle H^{(i)}\Big(\frac{a_{\textrm{\tiny FN}}}{\epsilon},\frac{2\,{\mathrm{i}}}{\epsilon\,\sqrt{v}}\Big)

\displaystyle=\mp\,\frac{{\mathrm{i}}}{\pi}\,\big(-\epsilon^{2}\,v\big)^{a_{\textrm{\tiny FN}}/2\,\epsilon}\,\Gamma\Big(\frac{a_{\textrm{\tiny FN}}}{\epsilon}\Big)\,\Big(1-\frac{1}{\epsilon\,(a_{\textrm{\tiny FN}}-\epsilon)}\,v^{-1}+O(v^{-2})\Big)

\displaystyle\quad\,\pm\frac{{\mathrm{i}}\,{\mathrm{e}}^{\mp\,\frac{\pi\,{\mathrm{i}}\,a_{\textrm{\tiny FN}}}{\epsilon}}\,\big(-\epsilon^{2}\,v\big)^{-a_{\textrm{\tiny FN}}/2\,\epsilon}}{\Gamma\big(\frac{a_{\textrm{\tiny FN}}}{\epsilon}+1\big)\sin\big(\frac{\pi\,a_{\textrm{\tiny FN}}}{\epsilon}\big)}\,\Big(1+\frac{1}{\epsilon\,(a_{\textrm{\tiny FN}}+\epsilon)}\,v^{-1}+O(v^{-2})\Big)

(5.47)

when
v\to\infty
. If we substitute
v=z/\Lambda^{2}
in the corresponding series expansion in
v^{-1}
for
g_{i}(v)
and reorganize the resulting expression as a series in
\Lambda^{2}
, we find

\displaystyle\psi_{i}(z,\Lambda)={\mathrm{e}}^{\pm\,\pi\,{\mathrm{i}}\,a_{\textrm{\tiny FN}}/2\,\epsilon}\,g_{i}(z/\Lambda^{2})=\sum_{k=0}^{\infty}\,\psi_{k,i}(z)\,\Lambda^{2k}\ ,

(5.48)

where
i\in\{1,2\}
; here
\psi_{0,i}(z)
obtains a contribution only from the leading term in the series expansion of
g_{0,i}(v)
when
v\to\infty
, while
\psi_{1,i}(z)
gets a contribution from the next-to-leading term in the series expansion of
g_{0,i}(v)
when
v\to\infty
as well as a contribution from the leading term in the series expansion of
g_{1,i}(v)
when
v\to\infty
, and so on. Note that
\psi_{1}(z,\Lambda)
is indeed asymptotically small near
z=0
when
{\rm arg}(\epsilon)=\frac{\pi}{2}
and
v
has negative real part as well as negative imaginary part.

Since the resulting function
\psi_{i}(z,\Lambda)
should be a solution of the original Mathieu equation (
5.24
), we should be able to express it as a linear combination of
\psi_{1}^{\prime\prime}(z,\Lambda)
and
\psi_{2}^{\prime\prime}(z,\Lambda)
, the eigenfunctions of the monodromy
\sf M
computed in (
5.27
). Indeed we find

\displaystyle\psi_{i}(z,\Lambda)=\frac{r_{i}}{\Lambda}\,\psi_{2}^{\prime\prime}(z,\Lambda)+\frac{s_{i}}{\Lambda}\,\psi_{1}^{\prime\prime}(z,\Lambda)\ ,

(5.49)

where

\displaystyle r_{i}

\displaystyle=\pm\,\frac{{\mathrm{i}}}{\pi}\,\Big(\frac{{\mathrm{i}}\,\Lambda}{\epsilon}\Big)^{\frac{a_{\textrm{\tiny FN}}}{\epsilon}}\,{\mathrm{e}}^{\pm\,\frac{\pi\,{\mathrm{i}}\,a_{\textrm{\tiny FN}}}{2\,\epsilon}}\,\Gamma\Big(-\frac{a_{\textrm{\tiny FN}}}{\epsilon}\Big)

\displaystyle\hskip 28.45274pt\times\Big(1-\frac{1}{\epsilon^{2}\,(\epsilon-a_{\textrm{\tiny FN}})^{2}}\,\Lambda^{4}+\frac{a_{\textrm{\tiny FN}}^{3}+a_{\textrm{\tiny FN}}^{2}\,\epsilon-29\,a_{\textrm{\tiny FN}}\,\epsilon^{2}+43\,\epsilon^{3}}{4\,\epsilon^{4}\,(\epsilon+a_{\textrm{\tiny FN}})\,(2\,\epsilon-a_{\textrm{\tiny FN}})^{2}\,(\epsilon-a_{\textrm{\tiny FN}})^{4}}\,\Lambda^{8}+O(\Lambda^{10})\Big)\ ,

\displaystyle s_{i}

\displaystyle=\pm\,\frac{{\mathrm{i}}}{\pi}\,\Big(\frac{\epsilon}{{\mathrm{i}}\,\Lambda}\Big)^{\frac{a_{\textrm{\tiny FN}}}{\epsilon}}\,{\mathrm{e}}^{\mp\,\frac{\pi\,{\mathrm{i}}\,a_{\textrm{\tiny FN}}}{2\,\epsilon}}\,\Gamma\Big(\frac{a_{\textrm{\tiny FN}}}{\epsilon}\Big)

(5.50)

\displaystyle\hskip 28.45274pt\times\Big(1-\frac{1}{\epsilon^{2}\,(\epsilon+a_{\textrm{\tiny FN}})^{2}}\,\Lambda^{4}+\frac{-a_{\textrm{\tiny FN}}^{3}+a_{\textrm{\tiny FN}}^{2}\,\epsilon+29\,a_{\textrm{\tiny FN}}\,\epsilon^{2}+43\,\epsilon^{3}}{4\,\epsilon^{4}\,(\epsilon-a_{\textrm{\tiny FN}})\,(2\,\epsilon+a_{\textrm{\tiny FN}})^{2}\,(\epsilon+a_{\textrm{\tiny FN}})^{4}}\,\Lambda^{8}+O(\Lambda^{10})\Big)\ ,

if we set the free parameters as
c_{1,\pm}^{(0)}=c_{3,\pm}^{(0)}=0
,
c_{2,\pm}^{(0)}=d_{2,\pm}^{(0)}
and
c_{4,\pm}^{(0)}=d_{4,\pm}^{(0)}
. This required computing
g_{i}(v)
up to order
\Lambda^{16}
. As a check, note that

\displaystyle{\sf M}\psi_{1}(z,\Lambda)

\displaystyle=-\frac{r_{1}\,{\mathrm{e}}^{-\pi\,{\mathrm{i}}\,a_{\textrm{\tiny FN}}/\epsilon}}{\Lambda}\,\psi_{2}^{\prime\prime}(z,\Lambda)-\frac{s_{1}\,{\mathrm{e}}^{\,\pi\,{\mathrm{i}}\,a_{\textrm{\tiny FN}}/\epsilon}}{\Lambda}\,\psi_{1}^{\prime\prime}(z,\Lambda)

\displaystyle=\frac{r_{2}}{\Lambda}\,\psi_{2}^{\prime\prime}(z,\Lambda)+\frac{s_{2}}{\Lambda}\,\psi_{1}^{\prime\prime}(z,\Lambda)

\displaystyle=\psi_{2}(z,\Lambda)\ .

(5.51)

The same strategy at the other end of
C
, where
z\to\infty
and it is useful to introduce a local coordinate
w=\Lambda^{2}\,z
, shows that

\displaystyle\psi_{i}^{\prime}(z,\Lambda)=\Lambda\,r_{[i+1]}\,\psi_{1}^{\prime\prime}(z,\Lambda)+\Lambda\,s_{[i+1]}\,\psi_{2}^{\prime\prime}(z,\Lambda)\ .

(5.52)

Indeed, we check that

\displaystyle{\sf M}\psi_{1}^{\prime}(z,\Lambda)

\displaystyle=-\Lambda\,r_{2}\,{\mathrm{e}}^{\,\pi\,{\mathrm{i}}\,a_{\textrm{\tiny FN}}/\epsilon}\,\psi_{1}^{\prime\prime}(z,\Lambda)-\Lambda\,s_{2}\,{\mathrm{e}}^{-\pi\,{\mathrm{i}}\,a_{\textrm{\tiny FN}}/\epsilon}\,\psi_{2}^{\prime\prime}(z,\Lambda)

\displaystyle=\Lambda\,r_{1}\,\psi_{1}^{\prime\prime}(z,\Lambda)+\Lambda\,s_{1}\,\psi_{2}^{\prime\prime}(z,\Lambda)

\displaystyle=\psi_{2}^{\prime}(z,\Lambda)\ .

(5.53)

We finally have all the ingredients necessary to compute the spectral coordinates
\mathcal{X}_{A}
and
\mathcal{X}_{B}
at
\nabla_{\epsilon}^{\rm oper}
, and thus to extract the effective twisted superpotential
\widetilde{\mathcal{W}}^{\text{eff}}
in a series expansion in
\Lambda
for the pure
SU(2)
theory at weak coupling. We find

\displaystyle\mathcal{X}_{A}(\nabla_{\epsilon}^{\rm oper})=-\exp\Big(-\pi\,{\mathrm{i}}\,\frac{a_{\textrm{\tiny FN}}}{\epsilon}\Big)\ ,

(5.54)

while

\displaystyle\mathcal{X}_{B}(\nabla_{\epsilon}^{\rm oper})

\displaystyle=\frac{\textrm{Wr}(\psi_{1}^{\prime},\psi_{1}^{\prime\prime})}{\textrm{Wr}(\psi_{1},\psi_{1}^{\prime\prime})}\,\frac{\textrm{Wr}(\psi_{1},\psi_{2}^{\prime\prime})}{\textrm{Wr}(\psi_{1}^{\prime},\psi_{2}^{\prime\prime})}=\frac{s_{1}\,s_{2}}{r_{1}\,r_{2}}\ ,

\displaystyle\mathcal{X}_{B}^{-}(\nabla_{\epsilon}^{\rm oper})

\displaystyle=\mathcal{X}_{A}^{2}\,\frac{s_{1}^{2}\,s_{2}^{2}}{(r_{1}\,s_{2}-r_{2}\,s_{1})^{2}}\ ,

\displaystyle\mathcal{X}_{B}^{+}(\nabla_{\epsilon}^{\rm oper})

\displaystyle=\mathcal{X}_{A}^{-2}\,\frac{(r_{1}\,s_{2}-r_{2}\,s_{1})^{2}}{r_{1}^{2}\,r_{2}^{2}}\ ,

(5.55)

where we used
s\wedge\tilde{s}=\epsilon\,\text{Wr}(\psi,\tilde{\psi})
.

From (
5.54
) we find

\displaystyle x=\frac{\epsilon}{\pi\,{\mathrm{i}}}\log(-\mathcal{X}_{A})=-a_{\textrm{\tiny FN}}\ .

(5.56)

Substituting (
5.4
) into (
5.4
) and expanding to order
\Lambda^{8}
we obtain

\displaystyle\frac{1}{2}\log\mathcal{X}_{B}

\displaystyle=-\frac{2\,a_{\textrm{\tiny FN}}}{\epsilon}\log\Big(\frac{\Lambda}{\epsilon}\Big)-\frac{1}{2}\log\frac{\Gamma\big(-\frac{a_{\textrm{\tiny FN}}}{\epsilon}\big)}{\Gamma\big(1+\frac{a_{\textrm{\tiny FN}}}{\epsilon}\big)}\,\frac{\Gamma\big(1-\frac{a_{\textrm{\tiny FN}}}{\epsilon}\big)}{\Gamma\big(\frac{a_{\textrm{\tiny FN}}}{\epsilon}\big)}+\frac{4\,a_{\textrm{\tiny FN}}\,\Lambda^{4}}{\epsilon\,(a_{\textrm{\tiny FN}}^{2}-\epsilon^{2})^{2}}

\displaystyle\quad\,+\frac{6\,(5\,a_{\textrm{\tiny FN}}^{5}-2\,a_{\textrm{\tiny FN}}^{3}\,\epsilon^{2}-37\,a_{\textrm{\tiny FN}}\,\epsilon^{4})}{\epsilon\,(a_{\textrm{\tiny FN}}^{2}-4\,\epsilon^{2})^{2}\,(a_{\textrm{\tiny FN}}^{2}-\epsilon^{2})^{4}}\,\Lambda^{8}+O(\Lambda^{10})\ ,

(5.57)

where we used

\displaystyle\frac{\Gamma\big(-\frac{a_{\textrm{\tiny FN}}}{\epsilon}\big)}{\Gamma\big(\frac{a_{\textrm{\tiny FN}}}{\epsilon}\big)}=-\frac{\Gamma\big(1-\frac{a_{\textrm{\tiny FN}}}{\epsilon}\big)}{\Gamma\big(1+\frac{a_{\textrm{\tiny FN}}}{\epsilon}\big)}\ .

(5.58)

The effective twisted superpotential is now obtained by integrating
\frac{1}{2}\log\mathcal{X}_{B}
with respect to
x=-a_{\textrm{\tiny FN}}
. This gives

\displaystyle\widetilde{\mathcal{W}}^{\text{eff}}(a_{\textrm{\tiny FN}};\Lambda;\epsilon)

\displaystyle=-\int\,\frac{1}{2}\log\mathcal{X}_{B}\ \mathrm{d}a_{\textrm{\tiny FN}}

\displaystyle=\frac{a_{\textrm{\tiny FN}}^{2}}{\epsilon}\log\Big(\frac{\Lambda}{\epsilon}\Big)-\frac{\epsilon}{2}\,\Upsilon\Big(-\frac{a_{\textrm{\tiny FN}}}{\epsilon}\Big)-\frac{\epsilon}{2}\,\Upsilon\Big(\frac{a_{\textrm{\tiny FN}}}{\epsilon}\Big)+\frac{2\,\Lambda^{4}}{\epsilon\,(a_{\textrm{\tiny FN}}^{2}-\epsilon^{2})}

\displaystyle\quad\,+\frac{5\,a_{\textrm{\tiny FN}}^{2}+7\,\epsilon^{2}}{\epsilon\,(a_{\textrm{\tiny FN}}^{2}-4\,\epsilon^{2})\,(a_{\textrm{\tiny FN}}^{2}-\epsilon^{2})^{3}}\,\Lambda^{8}+O(\Lambda^{10})\ ,

(5.59)

which indeed matches the effective twisted superpotential from (
17
) when we identify the monodromy parameter
a_{\textrm{\tiny FN}}
with the (
\epsilon
-corrected) Coulomb parameter
a
. As an additional check, we compute

\displaystyle-\epsilon\,\frac{\Lambda}{8}\,\frac{\partial\widetilde{\mathcal{W}}^{\text{eff}}}{\partial\Lambda}=-\frac{a_{\textrm{\tiny FN}}^{2}}{8}-\frac{\Lambda^{4}}{a_{\textrm{\tiny FN}}^{2}-\epsilon^{2}}-\frac{5\,a_{\textrm{\tiny FN}}^{2}+7\,\epsilon^{2}}{(a_{\textrm{\tiny FN}}^{2}-4\,\epsilon^{2})\,(a_{\textrm{\tiny FN}}^{2}-\epsilon^{2})^{3}}\,\Lambda^{8}+O(\Lambda^{10})=u\ ,

(5.60)

which agrees with the expansion of
u
in
\Lambda^{2}
given in (
5.34
). This is known as the quantum Matone relation
[
Ald+10
,
Dru+10
,
Ash+15
]
.

Finally, note that if we were to compute the effective twisted superpotential
\widetilde{\mathcal{W}}^{\text{eff}}
with respect to the spectral coordinates
(\mathcal{X}_{A},\mathcal{X}_{B}^{\pm})
, the result would only differ in the one-loop contributions. Instead of

\displaystyle\widetilde{\mathcal{W}}^{\rm eff}_{\textrm{1-loop}}(a_{\textrm{\tiny FN}};\epsilon)

\displaystyle=-\frac{\epsilon}{2}\,\Upsilon\Big(-\frac{a_{\textrm{\tiny FN}}}{\epsilon}\Big)-\frac{\epsilon}{2}\,\Upsilon\Big(\frac{a_{\textrm{\tiny FN}}}{\epsilon}\Big)

(5.61)

we would obtain

\displaystyle\widetilde{\mathcal{W}}^{{\rm eff},\pm}_{\textrm{1-loop}}(a_{\textrm{\tiny FN}};\epsilon)=\int\,\Big[\mp\log\Gamma\Big(\mp\,\frac{a_{\textrm{\tiny FN}}}{\epsilon}\Big)\mp\log\Gamma\Big(1\mp\frac{a_{\textrm{\tiny FN}}}{\epsilon}\Big)\Big]\,\mathrm{d}a_{\textrm{\tiny FN}}\ ,

(5.62)

since

\displaystyle\mathcal{X}^{-}_{B,\textrm{1-loop}}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{\pi^{2}}\,\Gamma\Big(\frac{a_{\textrm{\tiny FN}}}{\epsilon}\Big)^{2}\,\Gamma\Big(1+\frac{a_{\textrm{\tiny FN}}}{\epsilon}\Big)^{2}\ ,

\displaystyle\mathcal{X}^{+}_{B,\textrm{1-loop}}

\displaystyle=\frac{\pi^{2}}{\Gamma\big(-\frac{a_{\textrm{\tiny FN}}}{\epsilon}\big)^{2}\,\Gamma\big(1-\frac{a_{\textrm{\tiny FN}}}{\epsilon}\big)^{2}}\ .

(5.63)

Interestingly, these are the one-loop contributions to the Nekrasov partition function in the Nekrasov-Shatashvili limit, in the gauge theory or topological string scheme.
44
44

44

Recall that the one-loop contribution
\exp\widetilde{\mathcal{W}}^{\rm eff}_{\textrm{1-loop}}
is only determined up to a phase, see Footnote
25
.

5.5
E_{6}
Minahan-Nemeschansky Theory

The
E_{6}
Minahan-Nemeschansky theory, introduced in Section
3.2
, is an example of an intrinsically strongly coupled
{\mathcal{N}}=2
theory of class
\mathcal{S}
. Its Seiberg-Witten curve, given by (
5
), is a ramified triple covering
\Sigma\to C
over the three-punctured sphere with three maximal punctures (see Figure
25
B). The three maximal punctures correspond to an
SU(3)\times SU(3)\times SU(3)
flavour symmetry group in the ultraviolet, which is enhanced to
E_{6}
upon flowing to the infrared.

The
E_{6}
theory is superconformal in the massless limit where the Seiberg-Witten curve is given by the equation

\displaystyle w^{3}+\frac{u}{(z^{3}-1)^{2}}=0\ ,

(5.64)

which in this case defines a triple unramified covering over the sphere with three maximal punctures at the third roots of unity
z=1,\omega,\omega^{2}
, where
\omega={\mathrm{e}}^{\,2\pi\,{\mathrm{i}}/3}
. It has a one-dimensional Coulomb branch
\mathscr{B}
, parametrized by
u\in\mathbb{C}
. All points on the Coulomb branch are physically equivalent. In particular, there are no walls of marginal stability where BPS bound states can form or decay. The electromagnetic charge lattice
\Gamma
has three distinguished one-cycles, called
A
,
B
and
C
in Example
25
(see Figure
38
). The periods
\oint_{A}\,\lambda=\omega\,\oint_{B}\,\lambda=\omega^{2}\,\oint_{C}\,\lambda
are all of the same order, hence there is no canonical choice of an electric-magnetic duality frame.

In Example
5
we noted that the topology of the spectral network
\mathscr{W}(u,\vartheta)
only depends on the phase of the quantity
{\mathrm{e}}^{-3\,{\mathrm{i}}\,\vartheta}\,u
, and that the network seems to be “wild” at all phases
\vartheta
, except when

\displaystyle{\mathrm{e}}^{-{\mathrm{i}}\,\vartheta}\,\oint_{\gamma}\,\lambda\ \in\ \mathbb{R}

(5.65)

for some one-cycle
\gamma
on
\Sigma
, in which case the network is of higher rank Fenchel-Nielsen type. The simplest topology appears when
\gamma
is equal to either of the one-cycles
A
,
B
or
C
. It is illustrated in Figure
11
A for the choice
\gamma=A
. For convenience we have drawn this network again in Figure
41
. (The networks corresponding to
\gamma=B,C
are found simply by rotating this network through
120^{\circ}
.)

\begin{overpic}[E6CyclesV2.png]
\put(50.0,47.0){{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}$A$}}
\put(35.0,44.0){{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}$3$}}
\put(47.0,39.0){{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}$2$}}
\put(42.0,16.0){{\color[rgb]{0.1953,0.1953,1}$B$}}
\put(35.0,24.0){{\color[rgb]{0.1953,0.1953,1}$1$}}
\put(47.0,31.0){{\color[rgb]{0.1953,0.1953,1}$2$}}
\put(55.0,55.0){$23$}
\put(47.0,59.0){$32$}
\put(55.0,13.5){$13$}
\put(47.0,10.0){$31$}
\put(11.0,37.0){$12$}
\put(11.0,32.0){$21$}
\end{overpic}

Figure 41:
Higher rank Fenchel-Nielsen network for the
E_{6}
Minahan-Nemeschansky theory together with a choice of
A
-cycle and
B
-cycle.

The Fenchel-Nielsen coordinates
\mathcal{X}_{A}
and
\mathcal{X}_{B}
for the circular network
\mathscr{W}_{[1,0]}
illustrated in Figure
11
A have been obtained and studied in
[
HN20
]
, however in a form in which it is not easy to extract explicit expressions.

The effective twisted superpotential
\widetilde{\mathcal{W}}^{\text{eff}}
for the
E_{6}
theory is obtained by evaluating the coordinates
\mathcal{X}_{A}
and
\mathcal{X}_{B}
on the family of
T_{3}
-opers
\nabla_{\epsilon}^{\rm oper}(u)
written down in Example
22
.
Even though it is difficult to obtain exact expressions in
\epsilon
for this example, we have learned in Example
25
that the spectral coordinates
\mathcal{X}_{A}
and
\mathcal{X}_{B}
at
\nabla_{\epsilon}^{\rm oper}(u)
have an asymptotic expansion, as
\epsilon\to 0
in the half-plane with
{\rm Re}({\mathrm{e}}^{-{\mathrm{i}}\,\vartheta}\,\epsilon)\geq 0
, determined by the Riccati equation. With the expansion (
25
), and by defining
\widetilde{\mathcal{W}}^{\text{eff}}
through

\displaystyle x=\frac{\epsilon}{\pi\,{\mathrm{i}}}\log(-\mathcal{X}_{A})\qquad\mbox{and}\qquad\frac{\partial\widetilde{\mathcal{W}}^{\text{eff}}}{\partial x}=\frac{1}{2}\,\log\mathcal{X}_{B}\ ,

(5.66)

we find

\displaystyle\widetilde{\mathcal{W}}^{\text{eff}}(x;\epsilon)

\displaystyle=-\frac{\pi^{2}}{4\,\epsilon}\,{\mathrm{e}}^{\,4\pi\,{\mathrm{i}}/3}\,x^{2}+6\pi\,{\mathrm{i}}\,\epsilon\log\frac{x}{\epsilon}+\frac{12\,\sqrt{3}\,{\mathrm{i}}\,\epsilon^{3}}{x^{2}}-\frac{144\,{\mathrm{i}}\,\epsilon^{5}}{\pi\,x^{4}}

\displaystyle\quad\,+\frac{2\,\sqrt{3}\,\big(16800\pi^{13/2}+23\cdot 2^{2/3}\,\Gamma(\frac{1}{3})^{8}\,\Gamma(\frac{7}{6})^{5}\big)\,\epsilon^{7}}{35\pi^{17/2}\,x^{6}}+\cdots

(5.67)

in an expansion in
\epsilon
. Solving the challenge posed in Example
25
, that is, finding the Borel sum
\mathcal{S}_{\vartheta}\mathcal{X}_{A}(t)
, would enable one to write down the exact effective twisted superpotential
\widetilde{\mathcal{W}}^{\text{eff}}
in
\epsilon
for the
E_{6}
theory.

5.6
Derivation from Quantum Field Theory

Now that we have explained how the geometric recipe works, let us take a closer look at the physics behind it. We start by sketching a derivation of the NRS correspondence from the point of view of quantum field theory, mostly following the treatments of
[
NRS11
,
NW10
]
.

Consider a theory
\sf T=\sf T_{\mathfrak{g}}[C,\mathcal{D}]
of class
\mathcal{S}
in the
\frac{1}{2}\Omega
-background
\mathbb{R}^{2}_{\epsilon}\times S^{1}\times\mathbb{R}
. On the one hand, as we reviewed in Section
4.1
, Nekrasov and Shatashvili argued in
[
NS10
]
that the resulting theory
{\sf T}_{\epsilon}
in the infrared limit may be described as a two-dimensional sigma-model (with worldsheet
S^{1}\times\mathbb{R}
) into the complexified maximal torus of the gauge group
G
with the effective twisted superpotential
\widetilde{\mathcal{W}}^{\text{eff}}(a;\epsilon)
.

On the other hand, as we will describe here, an alternative three-dimensional sigma-model argument shows that this superpotential is also equal to the difference of the generating functions of two holomorphic Lagrangian submanifolds of the moduli space
\mathscr{M}^{\epsilon}_{\rm flat}(C,{}^{\textrm{\tiny L}}G_{\mathbb{C}})
of flat
{}^{\textrm{\tiny L}}G_{\mathbb{C}}

\epsilon
-connections on the Riemann surface
C
, in suitable Darboux coordinates. Pivotal in this argument is the observation in
[
NW10
]
that the precise metric on
\mathbb{R}_{\epsilon}^{2}
is not essential, as long as it is
U(1)
-invariant, so that we may equivalently replace it with a “cigar” metric of the form

\mathrm{d}s^{2}=\mathrm{d}r^{2}+f(r)\,\mathrm{d}\theta^{2}\ ,

(5.68)

with
0\leq r<\infty
and
0\leq\theta<2\pi
, where
f(r)\simeq r^{2}
for
r\rightarrow 0
and
f(r)\rightarrow\rho^{2}
as
r\rightarrow\infty
. We write
D
for
\mathbb{R}^{2}
endowed with such a cigar metric, and
D_{R}
for
D
restricted to
r\leq R
, with
R
larger than the radius
r_{0}
where the circle parametrized by
\theta
reaches its asymptotic radius
\rho
.

Since we take
R
to be very large,
D_{R}
looks globally like
I\times\widetilde{S}{\,}^{1}
, with the interval
I
parametrized by
0\leq r\leq R
and the circle
\widetilde{S}{\,}^{1}
by
\theta
. We then turn on a
\frac{1}{2}\Omega
-deformation with parameter
\epsilon
corresponding to the
U(1)
isometry of
D_{R}
generated by
\frac{\partial}{\partial\theta}
. We denote the resulting theory, that is, the theory
\sf T
in the
\frac{1}{2}\Omega
-background
D_{R,\epsilon}\times\mathbb{R}^{2}
, by the same symbol
{\sf T}_{\epsilon}
.

We now compactify the theory
{\sf T}_{\epsilon}
over the fiber
\widetilde{S}\,^{1}
of the cigar
D_{R,\epsilon}
. As demonstrated by
[
NW10
]
, the
\frac{1}{2}\Omega
-deformation can be undone away from the tip of the cigar, where
r=0
, in exchange for a field redefinition and setting the asymptotic radius to
\rho=|\epsilon|^{-1}
. Then, away from the tip of the cigar, compactification of the
\frac{1}{2}\Omega
-deformed theory
{\sf T}_{\epsilon}
gives the same result as compactification of the undeformed theory, but now with field and coupling redefinitions.

Following the arguments of Section
4.2
, we may thus describe the theory
{\sf T}_{\epsilon}
at low energies
E\ll|\epsilon|
, at least away from the tip of the cigar, as a three-dimensional
{\mathcal{N}}{=}4
sigma-model, whose target space is the hyperkähler Hitchin moduli space
\mathscr{M}_{\textrm{\tiny H}}(C,G)
. In
[
NW10
]
it is then argued that the complete result, after compactifying the theory
{\sf T}_{\epsilon}
over the fiber
\widetilde{S}\,^{1}
of the cigar and then T-dualizing along either circle
\widetilde{S}\,^{1}
or
S^{1}
,
45
45

45

Recall from Section
4.2
that to arrive at the conventional description of this sigma-model in terms of hypermultiplets (rather than linear multiplets), it is necessary to dualize the abelian gauge fields in order to obtain enough periodic scalars. Alternatively, one may perform T-duality on either fiber
S^{1}
or
\widetilde{S}\,^{1}
.
is a conventional three-dimensional
{\mathcal{N}}=4
sigma-model with worldvolume
I\times S^{1}\times\mathbb{R}
into either
\mathscr{M}_{\textrm{\tiny H}}(C,G)
or
\mathscr{M}_{\textrm{\tiny H}}(C,{}^{\textrm{\tiny L}}G)
(the Hitchin moduli space for the Langlands dual gauge group
{}^{\textrm{\tiny L}}G
). This sigma-model comes with two branes: a brane
\mathscr{L}^{\prime}
at
r=0
which arises purely from the geometry at the tip of the cigar, and a brane
\mathscr{L}
at
r=R
which descends from a choice of half-BPS boundary condition in the four-dimensional theory.

If we choose to T-dualize along the circle
\widetilde{S}\,^{1}
, the brane
\mathscr{L}^{\prime}
at
r=0
is the space-filling (or canonical) coisotropic brane from
[
KW07
]
. On the other hand, T-dualizing along the circle
S^{1}
results in its three-dimensional mirror brane, the brane of
\epsilon
-opers
\mathscr{L}_{\epsilon}^{\rm oper}
(also described in
[
KW07
]
and introduced in Section
4.3
of these notes). The latter is obviously the interesting choice for us.

As proposed in Section
5.2
, we assume that the brane
\mathscr{L}=\mathscr{L}_{\mathscr{W},\varPi}
at
r=R
is labelled by the distinguished spectral network
\mathscr{W}=\mathscr{W}(u,\vartheta_{\rm c})
together with a polarization
\varPi=\{A^{i},B_{i}\}
of the Seiberg-Witten curve
\Sigma_{u}
. That is, it corresponds to the holomorphic Lagrangian submanifold of the Hitchin moduli space defined by the equations

\displaystyle\mathcal{X}^{\mathscr{W}}_{B_{i}}=1

(5.69)

in the system of holomorphic exponentiated Darboux coordinates
\big\{\mathcal{X}^{\mathscr{W}}_{A^{i}},\mathcal{X}^{\mathscr{W}}_{B_{i}}\big\}
obtained by abelianization with respect to
\mathscr{W}
. The brane of
\epsilon
-opers
\mathscr{L}_{\epsilon}^{\rm oper}
is instead defined by its generating function
\mathcal{W}^{\rm oper}(\epsilon)
in these coordinates.
46
46

46

In order to preserve four supersymmetries in the three-dimensional sigma-model, it is necessary to choose
{\rm arg}(\epsilon)=\vartheta
. However, we can analytically continue all of our results, and in particular the generating function
\mathcal{W}^{\rm oper}(\epsilon)
, to any complex
\epsilon
. Indeed, recall from Section
4.4
that the spectral coordinates
\mathcal{X}_{\gamma}^{\mathscr{W}}
, evaluated on the family of flat
\epsilon
-connections
\nabla_{\epsilon}^{\rm oper}
, are equal to the Voros periods
\exp V_{\gamma}(\epsilon)
when
{\rm arg}(\epsilon)=\vartheta
, but then analytically continued to all
\epsilon\in\mathbb{C}^{\times}
.

This implies that, when the interval
I
is contracted to a point, the three-dimensional
{\mathcal{N}}=4
sigma-model with target space
\mathscr{M}_{\textrm{\tiny H}}(C,{}^{\textrm{\tiny L}}G)
may be equivalently described as a two-dimensional
{\mathcal{N}}=(2,2)
sigma-model with target space
(\mathbb{C}^{\times})^{r}
. This sigma-model is then characterized by a twisted superpotential
47
47

47

A similar statement holds in topologically twisted sigma-models, for example in the reduction of the two-dimensional A-model with worldsheet
I\times\mathbb{R}
to quantum mechanics on
\mathbb{R}
, where this twisted superpotential is the Morse function generating the Fukaya category, or in the three-dimensional Rozansky-Witten model, which is explained in
[
KRS09
]
. These arguments are generalized to three-dimensional
{\mathcal{N}}=4
gauge theories in
[
Bul+16
]
.

\displaystyle\widetilde{\mathcal{W}}{}^{\sigma}=\epsilon\,\mathcal{W}^{\rm oper}(\epsilon)\ .

(5.70)

Hence, comparing the two arguments, we conclude that the effective twisted superpotential
\widetilde{\mathcal{W}}^{\text{eff}}
characterizing the original
{\mathcal{N}}=2
field theory
{\sf T}_{\epsilon}
may be identified with the generating function
\mathcal{W}^{\rm oper}(\epsilon)
for the brane of
\epsilon
-opers
\mathscr{L}_{\epsilon}^{\rm oper}
. The vacua of the theory
{\sf T}_{\epsilon}
correspond to the points of intersection
\mathscr{L}_{\epsilon}^{\rm oper}\cap\mathscr{L}
.

Another check of the NRS correspondence would be to compare vacuum expectation values of half-BPS line defects in the theory
{\sf T}_{\epsilon}
. Suppose we consider such line defects wrapping the fiber
\widetilde{S}\,^{1}
of the cigar geometry
D_{R,\epsilon}
while being situated at a point of
\mathbb{R}^{2}
. In the ultraviolet these line defects are labelled by a loop or network on
C
as well as a phase
\zeta={\mathrm{e}}^{\,{\mathrm{i}}\,\vartheta}
(see e.g.
[
DMO09
,
GMN13
]
). Their vacuum expectation values in an infrared vacuum
u
have natural expansions

\displaystyle\langle L_{\zeta}\rangle_{u}=\sum_{\gamma\in\Gamma_{u}}\,\underline{\overline{\varOmega}}(\gamma,L_{\zeta};u)\ \mathcal{X}^{\mathscr{W}(u,\vartheta)}_{\gamma}\ ,

(5.71)

in terms of the spectral coordinates
\mathcal{X}_{\gamma}^{\mathscr{W}(u,\vartheta)}
obtained by abelianization with respect to the spectral network
\mathscr{W}(u,\vartheta)
. Here
\underline{\overline{\varOmega}}(\gamma,L_{\zeta};u)
are indices which count what are called the framed BPS states of charge
\gamma
supported by the line defect
L_{\zeta}
.

In order to preserve four supersymmetries in the theory
{\sf T}_{\epsilon}
, the phase
\zeta
of the line defect
L_{\zeta}
has to be chosen consistently with the boundary conditions
\mathscr{L}_{\epsilon}^{\rm oper}
and
\mathscr{L}_{\mathscr{W},\varPi}
(see Footnote
46
). The vacuum expectation value of the resulting half-BPS line defect
L_{\epsilon}
has an expansion

\displaystyle\langle L_{\epsilon}\rangle_{u}=\sum_{\gamma\in\Gamma_{u}}\,\underline{\overline{\varOmega}}(\gamma,L_{\epsilon};u)\ \mathcal{X}^{\mathscr{W}}_{\gamma}\ ,

(5.72)

in terms of the Darboux coordinates
